(g,e,h,k)\in G*E^{1}*G^{2}
, define a self-correspondence over
G
.
Moreover, up to an isomorphism every self-correspondence over
G
arises in this way, and the associated directed graph is determined up to isomorphism by the
correspondence.

Proof.

For the first part, the right action on
X=E^{1}*G
is well-defined and free as it is given by the right action of
G
on itself. The composition law for the left action on
X
is exactly the
1
-cocycle identity for the self-similar action.

Now, consider a self-correspondence over
G
on a set
X
. Put
E^{0}=G^{0}
and let
E^{1}\subseteq X
be a fundamental domain
E^{1}\subseteq X
for the orbit space
X/G
of the right
G
-action (that is
E^{1}
contains exactly one point from each of these orbits).
Then, together with anchor maps restricted from
X
to
E^{1}
, we get a directed graph
E=(E^{0},E^{1},\textbf{{r}},\textbf{{s}})
. Since the right
G
-action is free we get that

E^{1}*G\ni(e,g)\mapsto e\cdot g\in X
is an isomorphism of right
G
-sets. Under this isomorphism the right
G
-action on
X\cong E^{1}*G
is as described in the assertion.
For any
(g,e)\in G*E^{1}
there is a unique element in
\textbf{{s}}(ge)G
that we denote by
g|_{e}
such that
(ge)g|_{e}\in E^{1}
. Since the actions commute, the map
G*E^{1}\ni(g,e)\mapsto geg|_{e}\in E^{1}
is a left action of
G
on
E^{1}
. Using this left action on
E^{1}
, the left
G
-action on
X
, under the isomorphism
X\cong E^{1}*G
, is as described in the assertion. This forces the map
G*E^{1}\ni(g,e)\mapsto g|_{e}\in G
to satisfy the relations of Definition
4.1
.
∎

5.
The inverse semigroup analysis

In this section we analyse the inverse semigroup associated to a self-similar action
(G,E)
. We are mainly concerned with the properties described in Definition
3.13
. In particular, we generalise and improve upon a number of results
from
[
ExP17
,
EPS
,
Dea21
]
.

5.1.
The inverse semigroups and their partial order

Definition 5.1
.

The
inverse semigroup of the self-similar action

(G,E)
is

S(G,E)=E^{*}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}E^{*}\cup\{0\}=\left\{(\alpha,g,\beta):\alpha,\beta\in E^{*},g\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)G{\textbf{{s}}(\beta)}\right\}\cup\{0\}

with the multiplication given by

(\alpha,g,\beta)(\gamma,h,\delta)=\begin{cases}(\alpha(g\beta^{\prime}),g|_{\beta^{\prime}}h,\delta),&\text{if }\gamma=\beta\beta^{\prime},\\
(\alpha,g(h^{-1}|_{\gamma^{\prime}})^{-1},\delta(h^{-1}\gamma^{\prime})),&\text{if }\beta=\gamma\gamma^{\prime},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then
(\alpha,g,\beta)^{*}=(\beta,g^{-1},\alpha)
and every nonzero idempotent in
S(G,E)
is of the form

(\alpha,\textbf{{s}}(\alpha),\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in E^{*}
. We denote by
\mathcal{E}(G,E)\coloneqq\mathcal{E}(S(G,E))
the associated semilattice of idempotents.

The inverse semigroups
G\cup\{0\}
and
S(E)
discussed in Examples
3.20
and
3.21
are the extreme cases of Definition
5.1
. In the first one the graph
E
has no edges, and in the second one the groupoid
G
consists only of units.
In general, the map

(5.2)

E^{*}\ni\alpha\longmapsto f_{\alpha}\coloneqq(\alpha,\textbf{{s}}(\alpha),\alpha)\in\mathcal{E}(G,E)

yields a semigroup isomorphism
E^{*}\cup\{0\}\cong\mathcal{E}(G,E)
, cf. Example
3.21
.
The maps
S(E)\ni(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto(\alpha,\textbf{{s}}(\alpha),\beta)\in S(G,E)
and

G\ni g\mapsto(\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g))\in S(G,E)
determine embeddings
S(E)\hookrightarrow S(G,E)
and
G\cup\{0\}\hookrightarrow S(G,E)

of inverse semigroups from Examples
3.21
and
3.20
into
S(G,E)
, and these subsemigroups generate
S(G,E)
as a semigroup.

There is a natural
1
-cocycle map
c\colon S(G,E)\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{Z}
given by

(5.3)

c((\alpha,g,\beta))\coloneqq|\alpha|-|\beta|,\qquad(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)

that we call the
length cocycle
. The associated kernel inverse subsemigroup is

(5.4)

S_{0}(G,E)\coloneqq c^{-1}(0)\cup\{0\}=\{(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E):|\alpha|=|\beta|\}\cup\{0\}.

It contains the inverse subsemigroup generated by the idempotents
\mathcal{E}(G,E)
and the image of
G\cup\{0\}
in
S(G,E)
. This inverse semigroup is

S_{00}(G,E)\coloneqq\{(g\beta,g|_{\beta},\beta):(g,\beta)\in G*E^{*}\}\cup\{0\},

and it is isomorphic to the inverse semigroup
G*E^{*}\cup\{0\}
where

(g,\beta)(h,\alpha)\coloneqq\begin{cases}(gh,\alpha),&\text{if }h\alpha=\beta\beta^{\prime},\\
(gh,h^{-1}\beta),&\text{if }\beta=(h\alpha)\gamma^{\prime},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that
\mathcal{E}(G,E)\subseteq S_{00}(G,E)\subseteq S_{0}(G,E)\subseteq S(G,E)
, and so the partial order in
S_{00}(G,E)
and
S_{0}(G,E)
is the
one inherited from
S(G,E)
.

Lemma 5.5
.

We have
(\alpha,g,\beta)\leq(\gamma,h,\delta)
in
S(G,E)
if and only if
\beta=\delta\delta^{\prime}
,
\alpha=\gamma(h\cdot\delta^{\prime})
, and
g=h|_{\delta^{\prime}}
for some
\delta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\delta)E^{*}
, and so the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\cdot\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g=h|_{\delta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

commutes.
In particular, the order ideal generated by
(\gamma,h,\delta)
is

(\gamma,h,\delta)^{\downarrow}=\{\gamma(h\cdot\delta^{\prime}),h|_{\delta^{\prime}},\delta\delta^{\prime}):\delta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\delta)E^{*}\},

and
f_{\alpha}\leq(\gamma,h,\delta)
if and only if
\gamma=\delta
,
\alpha=\gamma\gamma^{\prime}
for some
\gamma^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\gamma)E^{*}
such that
h
strongly fixes
\gamma^{\prime}
in the sense that
h\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}
and
h|_{\gamma^{\prime}}=\textbf{{s}}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Since
(\alpha,g,\beta)^{*}(\alpha,g,\beta)=(\beta,\textbf{{s}}(\beta),\beta)
, we have
(\alpha,g,\beta)\leq(\gamma,h,\delta)
if and only if
the product
(\gamma,h,\delta)(\beta,\textbf{{s}}(\beta),\beta)
is equal to
(\alpha,g,\beta)
. The product is nonzero if either
\beta=\delta\delta^{\prime}
or
\delta=\beta\beta^{\prime}
.
If
\beta=\delta\delta^{\prime}
, the product evaluates to
(\gamma(h\cdot\delta^{\prime}),h|_{\delta^{\prime}},\delta\delta^{\prime})
, so it is equal to
(\alpha,g,\beta)
if and only if
the relations in the assertion hold. If
\delta=\beta\beta^{\prime}
,
the product evaluates to
(\gamma,h,\delta)
,
so it is equal to
(\alpha,g,\beta)
if and only if the relations in the assertion hold
for
\delta^{\prime}=\textbf{{s}}(\beta)
.

The remaining statements follow immediately from the first.
∎

The final two relations in Lemma
5.5
motivate the following definition.

Definition 5.6

(
[
ExP17
]
*Definition 5.2)
.

We say that
g\in G

strongly fixes

\alpha\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
or that
\alpha
is
strongly
g
-fixed
if

g\cdot\alpha=\alpha
and
g|_{\alpha}=\textbf{{s}}(\alpha).

If, in addition, no proper prefix of
\alpha
is strongly fixed by
g
we say that
\alpha
is a
minimal strongly
g
-fixed path
.

Remark 5.7
.

If
\alpha
is strongly
g
-fixed, then it is also strongly
g^{-1}
-fixed, because using (
4.11
)
we then have
g^{-1}|_{\alpha}=(g^{-1}|_{g\alpha})=(g|_{\alpha})^{-1}=\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)
.
A vertex
v\in E^{0}
is strongly
g
-fixed if and only if
g=v
, because we always have
g|_{v}=g
. A unit
x\in G^{0}
strongly fixes every path
\mu\in xE^{*}
as we always have
x\mu=\mu
and

x|_{\mu}=\textbf{{s}}(\mu)
.

The property of being strongly fixed respects the partial order on
E^{*}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
g\in G
,
\alpha\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
and
\beta\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}
.
The composed path
\alpha\beta
is strongly
g
-fixed if and only if
\alpha
is
g
-fixed and
\beta
is strongly
g|_{\alpha}
-fixed.
In particular, every extension of a strongly
g
-fixed path is strongly
g
-fixed, and so a path is strongly
g
-fixed if and only if it is an extension of a minimal strongly
g
-fixed path.

Proof.

Since
g(\alpha\beta)=(g\alpha)(g|_{\alpha}\beta)
, we see that
g
fixes
\alpha\beta
if and only if it fixes
\alpha
and
g|_{\alpha}
fixes
\beta
.
Combining this with
g|_{\alpha\beta}=(g|_{\alpha})|_{\beta}
gives the first part of the assertion.
If
\alpha
is strongly
g
-fixed, then
\beta
is trivially strongly fixed by
g|_{\alpha}=\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)
.
This implies the second part of the assertion.
∎

By Lemma
5.5
, the set of idempotents trivially fixed by
t=(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)
is given by

(5.9)

F_{t}=\begin{cases}\{f_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\colon g\text{ strongly fixes }\alpha^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}\}&\text{if }\alpha=\beta\\
\varnothing&\text{if }\alpha\neq\beta.\end{cases}

5.2.
Closedness

The following generalises
[
ExP17
]
*Theorem 12.2 and
[
EPS
]
*Theorem 4.2,
and characterises when the inverse semigroup considered is closed.

Proposition 5.10
.

The condition

(Fin)

every
g\in G
admits at most finitely many minimal strongly
g
-fixed paths;

is equivalent to each of the following:

(1)

the inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
is closed;

(2)

the inverse semigroup
S_{0}(G,E)
is closed;

(3)

the inverse semigroup
S_{00}(G,E)
is closed.

Proof.

Let us pick
t\in S(G,E)
and note that we may assume that
t=(\alpha,g,\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
, as otherwise
F_{t}=\varnothing
, by (
5.9
). Then denoting by
M_{g}
the finite set of all minimal strongly
g
-fixed paths, one sees
that the set

\{f_{\alpha\delta}\colon\delta\in M_{g}\}

is a cover of every
f_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\in F_{t}
. Indeed, by (
5.9
) the set
F_{t}
is parametrised by strongly
g
-fixed paths
\alpha^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
and for any such path there are
\delta\in M_{g}
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\delta)E^{*}
such that
\alpha^{\prime}=\delta\delta^{\prime}
,
and so
f_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\leq f_{\alpha\delta}
.
Hence,
(Fin)
implies
(1)
.

Implications
(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
\Rightarrow
(3)
are obvious.
Assume
(3)
and pick
g\in G
. We may assume that the set
M_{g}
of all minimal strongly
g
-fixed paths is non-empty.
Then we necessarily have
\textbf{{r}}(g)=\textbf{{s}}(g)
(it suffices that
g
fixes some path) and so
t\coloneqq(\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g))
is a valid element of
S_{00}(G,E)
.
By assumption there is a finite set
F\subseteq F_{t}
that covers
f_{\delta}
for every
\delta\in M_{g}
.
So for every
\delta\in M_{g}
there is
f_{\alpha^{\prime}}\in F
, where
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\delta
(
\alpha^{\prime}
is an extension of
\delta
).
Since
F
is finite so is
M_{g}
. Thus,
(3)
implies
(Fin)
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

Every
v\in G^{0}
is the unique minimal strongly fixed
v
-path. Hence,
in condition
(Fin)
we only need to look at
g\in G\setminus G^{0}
.
Also, by Lemma
5.8
, two minimal strongly
g
-fixed paths are different if they are incomparable.
Accordingly,
(Fin)
can be equivalently phrased as

(Fin)

every
g\in G
admits finitely many mutually incomparable strongly
g
-fixed paths.

Remark 5.12
.

Using Lemma
5.5
one may show that
(Fin)
is also equivalent to Hausdorffness of any of the inverse semigroups
S(G,E)
,
S_{0}(G,E)
or
S_{00}(G,E)
. We will prove it on the groupoid level, see Corollary
6.14
below.

5.3.
Quasi-fundamentalness and core subsemigroups

We begin by analysing the product
tf_{\gamma}t^{*}
for
t\in S(G,E)
and
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{E}(G,E)
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
t=(\alpha,g,\beta)
and
f_{\gamma}=(\gamma,\textbf{{s}}(\gamma),\gamma)
,
\gamma\in E^{*}
. Then

(5.14)

tf_{\gamma}t^{*}=\begin{cases}f_{\alpha(g\beta^{\prime})}&\text{if }\gamma=\beta\beta^{\prime},\\
f_{\alpha}&\text{if }\beta=\gamma\gamma^{\prime},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

In particular,
tf_{\gamma}t^{*}\cdot f_{\gamma}\neq 0
if and only if either
\gamma=\beta\beta^{\prime}
and
\gamma
is comparable with
\alpha(g\beta^{\prime})
; or

\beta=\gamma\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma
is comparable with
\alpha
.

Proof.

For the first statement we calculate

\displaystyle tft^{*}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(\alpha(g\beta^{\prime}),g|_{\beta^{\prime}},\beta\beta^{\prime})(\beta,g^{-1},\alpha)&\text{if }\gamma=\beta\beta^{\prime}\\
(\alpha,g,\gamma\gamma^{\prime})(\gamma\gamma^{\prime},g^{-1},\alpha)&\text{if }\beta=\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}=\begin{cases}(\alpha(g\beta^{\prime}),\textbf{{s}}(g\beta^{\prime}),\alpha(g\beta^{\prime}))&\text{if }\gamma=\beta\beta^{\prime}\\
(\alpha,\textbf{{s}}(\alpha),\alpha)&\text{if }\beta=\gamma\gamma^{\prime}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The second statement follows immediately from the first.
∎

It seems, to us, that the condition introduced in the next proposition has not appeared in the literature previously.
It was noticed in
[
Aak
]
that for a faithful self-similar action the inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
is always fundamental.

Proposition 5.15
.

The condition

(Evr)

if
g\in G
fixes every path in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
, then
g
strongly fixes some path in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
;

is equivalent to each of the following:

(1)

the inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
is quasi-fundamental;

(2)

the inverse semigroup
S_{0}(G,E)
is topologically free;

(3)

the inverse semigroup
S_{00}(G,E)
is topologically free.

The inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
is fundamental if and only if the self-similar action is faithful.

Proof.

Assume that
(Evr)
fails, and so there is
g\in G
with
v:=\textbf{{s}}(g)
that fixes every path in
vE^{*}
, but none of them is strongly
g
-fixed.
Put
t:=(v,g,v)\in S_{00}(G,E)
. Using (
5.14
), and that all paths in
vE^{*}
are fixed,
for any
\gamma\in vE^{*}
we get
tf_{\gamma}t^{*}=f_{\gamma}
. In particular,
f_{v}
is
t
-fixed but
F_{t}=\varnothing
by (
5.9
).
Hence
S_{00}(G,E)
, and all the more
S_{0}(G,E)
, are not topologically free.
Moreover, putting
s:=f_{v}
we see that
tf_{\gamma}t^{*}=sf_{\gamma}s^{*}
is either zero or
f_{\gamma}
if
\gamma\in vE^{*}
.
By Lemma
5.5
,
0\neq u\leq t=f_{v}
implies that
u=f_{v}
, and
f_{v}\leq s
implies that
g
strongly fixes
v
.
Hence
S(G,E)
is not quasi-fundamental.
This proves that any of the conditions
(1)
–
(3)
implies
(Evr)
.

Now assume
(Evr)
. We show that
S_{0}(G,E)
is topologically free. Let
t=(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S_{0}(G,E)
and fix
f_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{E}(G,E)
.
Thus we have
|\alpha|=|\beta|
and
tf_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}t^{*}\cdot f_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\neq 0
for every
\gamma^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\gamma)E^{*}
. Therefore, the second part of Lemma
5.13
implies that

\alpha=\beta
and
g
fixes all paths in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
.
Hence there is a strongly
g
-fixed path
\gamma^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
by
(Evr)
. If
|\gamma|\leq|\alpha|
, then
\delta:=\alpha\gamma^{\prime}\in F_{t}
, by (
5.9
), and
f_{\delta}f_{\gamma}=f_{\gamma}\neq 0
.
Assume then that
|\gamma|\geq|\alpha|
so that
\alpha\alpha^{\prime}=\gamma
. Then
g|_{\alpha^{\prime}}
fixes all paths in
\textbf{{s}}(\gamma)E^{*}
. Hence there is
a strongly
g|_{\alpha^{\prime}}
-fixed path
\gamma^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
by
(Evr)
. Then
\alpha^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}
is
g
-strongly fixed by Lemma
5.8
.
Thus for
\delta:=\gamma\gamma^{\prime}=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}
we have
\delta\in F_{t}
and
f_{\delta}f_{\gamma}=f_{\delta}\neq 0
.
Accordingly,
S_{0}(G,E)
is topologically free, and so all the more
S_{00}(G,E)
is topologically free.

Finally, we show that
(Evr)
implies that
S(G,E)
is quasi-fundamental. Take two elements
t=(\alpha,g,\beta)
and
s=(\eta,h,\delta)
in
S(G,E)
such that

tf_{\gamma}t^{*}=sf_{\gamma}s^{*}
for every
\gamma\in E^{*}
. In view of (
5.14
), the equalities
tf_{\beta}t^{*}=f_{\alpha}=sf_{\beta}s^{*}
and
tf_{\eta}t^{*}=f_{\delta}=sf_{\eta}s^{*}
imply
that we have
\alpha=\eta
and
\beta=\delta
. Therefore for any
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}=\textbf{{s}}(h)E^{*}
the equality

tf_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}t^{*}=sf_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}s^{*}
means that
g\beta^{\prime}=h\beta^{\prime}
. In other words,
g^{-1}h
fixes all paths in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
. Hence there is
a
g^{-1}h
-strongly fixed
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
by
(Evr)
. Equality
g^{-1}h|_{\beta^{\prime}}=\textbf{{s}}(\beta)
is equivalent to
g|_{\beta^{\prime}}=h|_{\beta^{\prime}}
, and so

(\alpha g\beta^{\prime},g|_{\beta^{\prime}},\beta\beta^{\prime})=(\alpha h\beta^{\prime},h|_{\beta^{\prime}},\beta\beta^{\prime})
. Denoting this element by
u
we have
0\neq u\leq s,t
by Lemma
5.5
.

S(G,E)
is quasi-fundamental.

This shows that
(Evr)
implies the conditions
(1)
–
(3)
. The above argument also shows
that
S(G,E)
is fundamental if and only if every
g\in G
that fixes all paths in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
has to be a unit, that is
(G,E)
is faithful.
∎

Example 5.16
.

Any action of a group
\Gamma
by automorphisms of a graph
E
may be viewed as a self-similar action of
\Gamma
on
E
with
the trivial
1
-cocycle
\Gamma\times E^{1}\to\Gamma
where
g|_{e}=g
for all
g\in\Gamma
and
e\in E^{1}
.
This in turn may be treated as a self-similar action of the transformation groupoid
G=\Gamma\times E^{1}
, see Example
4.3
.
Such actions satisfy
(Fin)
. They were used in
[
LR-Hb
]
to model crossed products for group actions on graph algebras by

C^{*}
-algebras associated to the self-similar action.
For such an action condition
(Evr)
holds if and only if for every
g\in\Gamma\setminus\{1\}
and every
v\in E^{0}

there is a path
\gamma\in vE^{*}
that contains an edge not fixed by
g
.

5.4.
Topological freeness and effectiveness

We now turn to description of topological freeness of
S(G,E)
, which generalises topological freeness of
S(E)
described in Example
3.21
.

Definition 5.17

(cf.
[
ExP17
]
*Definition 14.1)
.

For any
g\in G
, a
g
-cycle
is a finite path
\alpha\in E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
such that
g\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)=\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)
. A
G
-cycle
is a path that is a
g
-cycle for some
g\in G
.

Remark 5.18
.

Cycles in
E
are nothing but
g
-cycles for
g\in G^{0}
. In general,
\textbf{{r}}(g)E^{*}\textbf{{s}}(g)\setminus E^{0}
is the set of
g
-cycles and
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}\textbf{{r}}(g)\setminus E^{0}
is the set of
g^{-1}
-cycles.

Lemma 5.19
.

A path
\alpha\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
has an entrance if and only if

g\alpha
has an entrance.

Proof.

Write
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}
, where each
\alpha_{i}\in E^{1}
.
As the action of
G
on
E^{*}
respects the length of paths, we get
g\alpha=\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}
, where each
\beta_{i}\in E^{1}
, and
|\textbf{{r}}(\alpha_{i})E^{1}|=|\textbf{{r}}(\beta_{i})E^{1}|
for all
i
.
∎

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
\alpha
be a
g
-cycle. Let
\alpha_{1}\coloneqq\alpha
and
g_{1}\coloneqq g^{-1}
, and recursively define

\alpha_{n+1}\coloneqq g_{n}\alpha_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{n+1}\coloneqq g_{n}|_{\alpha_{n}}\qquad\text{ for $n>1$}.

Then the concatenation
\alpha_{\infty}\coloneqq\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\cdots
yields a well-defined infinite path.
Moreover, if
\alpha
has no entrance, then so does
\alpha_{\infty}
, and

\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)E^{*}
consists of finite subpaths of
\alpha_{\infty}
.

Proof.

The first part is straightforward. If
\alpha
has no entrance, then every
\alpha_{n}
has no entrance, by Lemma
5.19
,
and so
\alpha_{\infty}
has no entrance. If there is a path
\gamma\in\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)E^{*}
which is not a subpath of
\alpha_{\infty}
, then we may write
\gamma
as
\gamma=\mu\gamma^{\prime}

where
\mu
is the longest common subpath of
\gamma
and
\alpha_{\infty}\in\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)E^{\infty}
. Then
\textbf{{s}}(\mu)
is a base point of
\alpha_{\infty}
that receives at least two different edges, and so
\alpha_{\infty}
has an entrance.
∎

Definition 5.21

(cf.
[
ExP17
]
*Definition 14.9)
.

We say that
g\in G

has slack
if there
is a finite set
F\subseteq\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
consisting of strongly
g
-fixed paths and such that every path in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
is comparable with a path in
F
.

Remark 5.22
.

Every unit
g\in G^{0}
has slack. Since extensions of strongly fixed paths are strongly fixed (see Lemma
5.8
),
if
E^{1}
is finite, then
g\in G
has slack if and only if there
is
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that every
\gamma\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
with
|\gamma|\geq n
is strongly fixed by
g
. Thus, our definition is consistent with
[
ExP17
]
*Definition 14.9 formulated for finite graphs.

Proposition 5.23
.

In addition to condition
(Evr)
from Proposition
5.15
, consider the following two more conditions:

(Cyc)

for every
g\in G
every
g
-cycle has an entrance;

(Slack)

if
g\in G
fixes every path in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
, then
g
has slack.

Then

(1)

S(G,E)
is topologically free if and only if
(Cyc)
and
(Evr)
hold;

(2)

S(G,E)
is effective if and only if
(Cyc)
and
(Slack)
hold.

Proof.

To show sufficiency of the above conditions, suppose that
t=(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)
fixes
f_{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon,\textbf{{s}}(\varepsilon),\varepsilon)
. For any
\gamma\in E^{*}
recall that
f_{\gamma}\leq f_{\varepsilon}
if and only if
\gamma\leq\varepsilon
. For any
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}=\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{*}
,
putting
\gamma\coloneqq\beta\beta^{\prime}
the relation
(tf_{\gamma}t^{*})\cdot f_{\gamma}\neq 0
is equivalent to saying
that
\beta\beta^{\prime}
is comparable with
\alpha(g\beta^{\prime})
(see Lemma
5.13
). By passing to
t^{*}
if necessary we may assume that
|\alpha|\leq|\beta|
(see Remark
3.14
).

First suppose that
|\alpha|<|\beta|
. Then
\beta=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
and the comparability condition says that and

\beta\beta^{\prime}=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
is an extension of
\alpha(g\beta^{\prime})
for every
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}=\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{*}=\textbf{{s}}(\alpha^{\prime})E^{*}
.
In particular,

\textbf{{r}}(\alpha^{\prime})=\textbf{{r}}(g\beta^{\prime})=g\textbf{{r}}(\beta^{\prime})=g\textbf{{s}}(\alpha^{\prime}),

and so
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
g
-cycle. Thus, assuming
(Cyc)
,
\alpha^{\prime}
has an entrance and so there is
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\textbf{{r}}(\alpha^{\prime})E^{*}
incomparable with
\alpha
. Putting
\beta^{\prime}\coloneqq g^{-1}\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{*}
we get that
\beta\beta^{\prime}=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}
can not be an extension of
\alpha(g\beta^{\prime})=\alpha\alpha^{\prime\prime}
, which is a contradiction. Thus, condition
(Cyc)
excludes the case
|\alpha|<|\beta|
.

Suppose then that
|\alpha|=|\beta|
. Then comparability of
\beta\beta^{\prime}
and
\alpha(g\beta^{\prime})
means that
\alpha=\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}=g\beta^{\prime}
. In particular,
g
fixes every
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
.
Consider two subcases.

Assume
\varepsilon\leq\beta
. If
(Evr)
holds there exists a strongly
g
-fixed
\overline{\beta}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
.
Putting
\gamma\coloneqq\beta\overline{\beta}
, we have
f_{\gamma}\in F_{t}
by (
5.9
), and

f_{\gamma}\cdot f_{\varepsilon}=f_{\gamma}\neq 0
. Similarly, if we assume
(Slack)
, then there is a finite
F\subseteq\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}

such that
\{f_{\beta\overline{\beta}}:\overline{\beta}\in F\}\subseteq F_{t}
covers
f_{\varepsilon}
.

Assume
\beta\leq\varepsilon
, then we have
\varepsilon=\beta\beta^{\prime}
for some
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
. Note that every
\beta^{\prime\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta^{\prime})E^{*}
is fixed by
g|_{\beta^{\prime}}
as
we have
\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}=g(\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime})=g\beta^{\prime}g|_{\beta^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime\prime}=\beta^{\prime}g|_{\beta^{\prime}}\beta^{\prime\prime}
. Hence, if we assume
(Evr)
there is a strongly
g|_{\beta^{\prime}}
-fixed
\overline{\beta}\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta^{\prime})E^{*}=\textbf{{s}}(\varepsilon)E^{*}
. Then
\beta^{\prime}\overline{\beta}
is strongly
g
-fixed because
g|_{\beta^{\prime}\overline{\beta}}=(g|_{\beta^{\prime}})|_{\overline{\beta}}=\textbf{{s}}(\overline{\beta})
. Putting
\gamma\coloneqq\varepsilon\overline{\beta}
, we get
f_{\gamma}\in F_{t}
by (
5.9
), and

f_{\gamma}\cdot f_{\varepsilon}=f_{\gamma}\neq 0
.
Similarly, if we assume
(Slack)
, then there is a finite
F\subseteq\textbf{{s}}(\varepsilon)E^{*}

such that
\{f_{\varepsilon\overline{\beta}}:\overline{\beta}\in F\}\subseteq F_{t}
covers
f_{\varepsilon}
.

This finishes the proof of sufficiency of
(Cyc)
and
(Evr)
for topological freeness, and
(Cyc)
and
(Slack)
for effectiveness of
S(G,E)
.
To check the necessity suppose first that
(Cyc)
fails, and so there is
g
-cycle
\alpha
without an entrance. Putting
t\coloneqq(\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\alpha)\in S(G,E)
we get from (
5.9
) that
F_{t}=\varnothing
. We claim that
t
fixes
f_{\alpha}\coloneqq(\alpha,\textbf{{s}}(\alpha),\alpha)
which provides a contradiction to topological freeness (and hence all the more to effectivness) of
S(G,E)
.
Indeed, by Lemma
5.20
every path in
\textbf{{r}}(g)E^{*}
is a prefix of a (unique) infinite path
\alpha_{\infty}\in\textbf{{r}}(g)E^{\infty}
.
In other words all paths in
\textbf{{r}}(g)E^{*}
are comparable and so for
\gamma,\beta\in\textbf{{r}}(g)E^{*}
we have
f_{\gamma}\leq f_{\beta}
if and only if

|\gamma|\geq|\beta|
. Thus, if
f_{\gamma}\leq f_{\alpha}
, that is if
\gamma=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}
, then we get

tf_{\gamma}t^{*}\cdot f_{\gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:pre_fixed_characterisation}}}{{=}}f_{g\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot f_{\gamma}=f_{g\alpha^{\prime}}\neq 0
, because
|\gamma|\geq|g\alpha^{\prime}|
. This proves the claim.

Now suppose that
(Evr)
fails, so that there is
g\in G
that fixes all paths in
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
but does not strongly fix any of them.
This implies that
t\coloneqq(\textbf{{s}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g))\in S(G,E)
fixes all idempotents
f_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
(see Lemma
5.13
) and
F_{t}=\varnothing
(see (
5.9
)), so
S(G,E)
is not topologically free.
Similarly, if
(Slack)
fails, there is
g\in G
such that
t\coloneqq(\textbf{{s}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g))\in S(G,E)
fixes idempotents
f_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}
but for every finite
F\subseteq\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
consisting of strongly
g
-fixed paths there is a path in
\alpha\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{*}
which is not comparable with any of paths in
F
.
The latter says that every finite
F\subseteq F_{t}
does not cover
f_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}
, so
S(G,E)
is not effective.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

Proposition
5.23
can be viewed as a far-reaching generalisation of
[
ExP17
]
*Theorem 14.10
which aimed at characterisation of effectiveness of the associated tight groupoid
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
.
We show in Example
6.29
below that, in general, effectiveness of
S(G,E)
is strictly weaker than that of
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
.

5.5.
Minimality

To characterise minimality, we combine the path preorder relation
v\leftarrow w
on
E^{0}=G^{0}
(that is
vE^{*}w\neq\varnothing
, see Example
3.21
) with the orbit equivalence relation
v\sim w
in the groupoid
G
(that is
vGw\neq\varnothing
).

Definition 5.25
.

For
v,w\in E^{0}
we write
v\ll w
if there exists
v^{\prime}\in E^{0}
such that
vE^{*}v^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
and
v^{\prime}Gw\neq\varnothing
.
We say that
(G,E)
is
cofinal
if the set of base points of any boundary path
\mu=\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\in\partial E
is cofinal in
(E^{0},\ll)
, that is for every
v\in E^{0}
there is
i
such that
v\ll\textbf{{s}}(\mu_{i})
.

Remark 5.26
.

The relation
\ll
is a generalisation of the relation introduced in
[
ExP17
]
*Definition 13.3, and cofinality of
(G,E)
is a generalisation of
a condition called
weak
\Gamma
-transitivity
in
[
ExP17
]
*Definition 13.4.

The relation
\ll
is natural from the point of view of the Zappa–Szép product category

E^{*}\bowtie G
, cf. Remark
4.22
.
Indeed, we have
v\ll w
if and only if there is an arrow from
w
to
v
in
E^{*}\bowtie G
.
In particular, this immediately implies that
\ll
is a preorder, which was an issue in
[
ExP17
]
. More specifically,
v\ll w
is equivalent to existence of a pair
(\alpha,g)\in v(E^{*}\bowtie G)w
, which means that the relations
v=\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)
and
\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)=gw
make sense
and hold (here
\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)=\textbf{{r}}(g)
plays the role of
v^{\prime}
in Definition
5.25
). This is weaker than assuming existence of a pair
(g,\alpha)\in vG*E^{*}w
, which means that the relations
v=g\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)
and
\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)=w
make sense
and hold. Indeed, if
(g,\alpha)\in vG*E^{*}w
, then we can consider it as a composable pair in
E^{*}\bowtie G
, namely
((v,g),(\alpha,w))
, and the composition in this category gives
(v,g)(\alpha,w)=(g\alpha,g|_{\alpha})\in E^{*}\bowtie G=E^{*}*G
.
Pictorially, for any vertices
v,w\in E^{0}
we have the following implication

(5.27)

\exists_{(g,\alpha)}\,\,\hbox to93.3pt{\vbox to41.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 46.6503pt\lower-20.7639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.52007pt}{17.51253pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{

{{{{{}}{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{29.87238pt}{-0.8264pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

where the consequent means that
v\ll w
. In the context of Exel-Pardo group actions the implication (
5.27
) can be reversed, see
[
ExP17
]
*Proposition 13.2, and so it does not matter which condition one uses. In our general context, it is important to use the weaker condition, and implication (
5.27
) is exactly what one needs to prove the following.

Lemma 5.28
.

The relation
\ll
is the smallest preorder relation on
E^{0}
containing the path preorder relation
\leftarrow
and orbit equivalence
\sim
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\ll
contains
\leftarrow
and
\sim
, and every transitive relation on
E^{0}
containing
\leftarrow
and
\sim
necessarily contains
\ll
.
Thus, it suffices to show that
\ll
is transitive, which follows from the above description as coming from morphisms in the Zappa–Szép category. It can also be readily proved using (
5.27
).
∎

We use the relation
\ll
to describe the conjugacy classes of idempotents in
S(G,E)
.
For instance, Lemma
5.13
says that for every
v\in E^{0}
we have

(5.29)

\{tf_{v}t^{*}:t\in S(G,E)\}\cup\{0\}=\{f_{\alpha}:\alpha\in E^{*},\,\,v\ll\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)\}\cup\{0\},

which is crucial in the proof of the following

Proposition 5.30
.

The inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
is minimal if and only if
(G,E)
is cofinal.

Proof.

Suppose that
S(G,E)
is minimal. Fix
v\in E^{0}
and
\mu=\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\in\partial E
. We identify
\mathcal{E}(G,E)
with
E^{*}\cup\{0\}
.
If
\mu\in\partial E\setminus E^{\infty}
, then
\textbf{{s}}(\mu)
is either a source or an infinite receiver. Thus, a finite set
T_{0}\subseteq E^{*}
covers
\mu
in
E^{*}\cup\{0\}
if and only if
T_{0}
contains a prefix of
\mu
. Similarly, if
\mu\in E^{\infty}
and
T_{0}
covers a prefix of
\mu
, then
T_{0}
has to contain a prefix of
\mu
.
By (
5.14
), for every finite
T\subseteq S(G,E)
the set
\{tf_{v}t^{*}:t\in T\}
(modulo the zero element) is of the form
\{f_{\alpha}:\alpha\in T_{0}\}

for a finite
T_{0}\subseteq E^{*}
such that
v\ll\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in T_{0}
.
Thus, applying minimality of
S(G,E)
to
f_{v}
and
f_{\textbf{{s}}(\mu)}
, if
\mu\notin E^{\infty}
, or to
f_{v}
and
f_{\mu^{\prime}}
for some prefix
\mu^{\prime}
of
\mu
, if
\mu\in E^{\infty}
,
we conclude that there is
i
such that
v\ll\textbf{{s}}(\mu_{i})
.
This shows that minimality of
S(G,E)
implies cofinality of
(G,E)
.

Conversely, suppose that
(G,E)
is cofinal. Fix
\gamma,\mu\in E^{*}
and put
v\coloneqq\textbf{{s}}(\gamma)
and
w\coloneqq\textbf{{s}}(\mu)
.
We show that
for any
T\subseteq wE^{*}
such that
v\ll\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in T
, we have

\{f_{\mu\alpha}:\alpha\in T\}\subseteq\{tf_{\gamma}t^{*}:t\in S(G,E)\}.

Indeed, if
u\in E^{0}
,
\beta\in vE^{*}u
, and
g\in uG\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)
, then putting
t\coloneqq(\mu\alpha,g,\gamma\beta)
we get
tf_{\gamma}t^{*}=f_{\mu\alpha}
by (
5.14
).

We identify a finite
T
as above such that
\{\mu\alpha:\alpha\in T\}
covers
\mu
. If
v\ll w
then
T\coloneqq\{w\}
does the job.
So suppose that
v\not\ll w
. By cofinality,
w
is regular and so the set
wE^{1}
is finite non-empty. In particular, the sets
F_{1}\coloneqq\{e\in wE^{1}:v\ll\textbf{{s}}(e)\}

and
G_{1}\coloneqq\{e\in wE^{1}:v\not\ll\textbf{{s}}(e)\}
are finite.
In general, for
n\geq 1
the sets

\displaystyle F_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\in wE^{n}:v\ll\textbf{{s}}(\alpha_{n})\text{ and }v\not\ll\textbf{{s}}(\alpha_{k})\text{ for all }k=1,\dots,n-1\},\text{ and }

\displaystyle G_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\in wE^{n}:v\not\ll\textbf{{s}}(\alpha_{k})\text{ for all }k=1,\dots,n\},

are finite, as
\textbf{{s}}(G_{n})
consists of regular elements and every element in
G_{n+1}\cup F_{n+1}
is an extension of an element in
G_{n}
.
By cofinality there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
G_{N}=\varnothing
as otherwise there would be an infinite path
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\cdots
such that
v\not\ll\textbf{{s}}(\alpha_{k})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
For this
N
, every element in
wE^{*}
is comparable with a path in the finite set
T\coloneqq\bigcup_{k=1}^{N}F_{k}
. Hence,

\{\mu\alpha:\alpha\in T\}
covers
\mu
, and equivalently
\{f_{\mu\alpha}:\alpha\in T\}
covers
\mu
. So
S(G,E)
is minimal.
∎

The appropriate notion of an invariant subset of
E^{0}
for the self-similar action
(G,E)
is a set which is
G
-invariant, hereditary and saturated (cf. Examples
3.21
).

Definition 5.31
.

We say that a subset
V\subseteq E^{0}
is
(G,E)
-invariant
if it is
G
-invariant, hereditary and saturated.

Note that
V\subseteq E^{0}
is
G
-invariant and hereditary if and only if it is
\ll
upward closed, so this condition could be viewed as a positive invariance.
Similarly,
V\subseteq E^{0}
is
G
-invariant and saturated if and only if
V
contains every regular vertex
w\in E^{0}
for which there exists
g\in Gw
such that
gwE^{1}\subseteq E^{1}V
,
which could be viewed as a negative invariance.

Lemma 5.32
.

Fix
v\in E^{0}
and let
H_{0}(v)\coloneqq\{w\in E^{0}:v\ll w\}
. For
n\geq 1
inductively define
H_{n+1}(v)
as the union of
H_{n}(v)
and
G
-orbits of all those regular vertices

w\in E^{0}
such that
\textbf{{s}}(e)\in H_{n}(v)
for all
e\in wE^{1}
. Then every
H_{n}(v)
is
\ll
upward closed and
H(v)\coloneqq\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}H_{n}(v)
is the smallest
(G,E)
-invariant set containing
v
.

Proof.

Since
H_{0}(v)
is upwards closed, it suffices to prove that if
H_{n}(v)
is
\ll
upward closed, then so is
H_{n+1}(v)
.
Take any
u\in H_{n+1}(v)
and
w\in E^{0}
such that
u\ll w
. If
u\in H_{n}(v)
, then
w\in H_{n}(v)\subseteq H_{n+1}(v)
because
H_{n}(v)
is upward closed.
Otherwise,
u
is regular and
uE^{1}\subseteq E^{1}H_{n}(v)
. Take
(\alpha,g)\in E^{*}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}G
so that
u=\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)
and
g\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)=w
.
If
|\alpha|\geq 1
, then
w\in H_{n}(v)\subseteq H_{n+1}(v)
. Otherwise
\alpha
is a vertex, and so
gu=w
, which gives
w\in H_{n+1}(v)
by construction.
∎

Proposition 5.33
.

The self-similar action
(G,E)
is cofinal if and only if there are no nontrivial
(G,E)
-invariant sets in
E^{0}
.

Proof.

Assume that
(G,E)
is not cofinal. There two cases to consider.
Firstly, there might be a singular vertex
w\in E^{0}
and a vertex
v\in E^{0}
such that
v\not\ll w
. By Lemma
5.32
,
w\not\in H_{0}(v)
, and since
w
is singular, we inductively get
that
w\not\in H_{n}(v)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,
w\not\in H(v)
, and so
H(v)
is a nontrivial
(G,E)
-invariant set.
Secondly, it may happen that there is an infinite path
\mu\in E^{\infty}
and a vertex
v\in E^{0}
such that
v\not\ll w
for every
w
in the set
B_{\mu}=\{\textbf{{s}}(\mu_{i})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
of base points of
\mu=\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots
.
This implies that
H_{0}(v)\cap B_{\mu}=\varnothing
, and since
\textbf{{s}}(\mu_{i+1})\not\in H_{0}(v)
implies
\textbf{{s}}(\mu_{i})=\textbf{{r}}(\mu_{i+1})\not\in H_{1}(v)
, we also have
H_{1}(v)\cap B_{\mu}=\varnothing
.
Proceeding inductively,
H_{n}(v)\cap B_{\mu}=\varnothing
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,
H(v)
is a nontrivial
(G,E)
-invariant set.

Conversely, assume that
(G,E)
is cofinal. Seeking a contradiction, suppose that there is a nontrivial
(G,E)
-invariant set
V\subseteq E^{0}
. By cofinality,
and since
V
is
\ll
-upward closed,
V
contains all singular vertices and intersects the set of base points of every infinite path. Take any
w\in E^{0}\setminus V
. Then
w
is necessarily regular, and so the sets
F_{1}\coloneqq wE^{1}V

and
G_{1}\coloneqq wE^{1}\setminus V
are finite. The set
G_{1}
is non-empty because
V
is hereditary and
w\not\in V
.
In general, for
n\geq 1
we let

\displaystyle F_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\in wE^{n}:\textbf{{s}}(\alpha_{n})\in V\text{ and }\textbf{{s}}(\alpha_{k})\not\in V\text{ for all }k=1,\dots,n-1\},\text{ and }

\displaystyle G_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\in wE^{n}:\textbf{{s}}(\alpha_{k})\not\in V\text{ for all }k=1,\dots,n\}.

Then
\textbf{{s}}(G_{n})
consists of regular elements and
G_{n+1}\cup F_{n+1}
is the set of one-edge extensions of elements in
G_{n}
.
In particular, every
G_{n+1}
is non-empty. Indeed, if
G_{n+1}=\varnothing
, then
F_{n+1}
is the set of all one edge extensions of elements in
G_{n}
,
and so
\textbf{{s}}(G_{n})E^{1}\subseteq E^{1}\textbf{{s}}(F_{n+1})\subseteq E^{1}V
which implies
\textbf{{s}}(G_{n})\subseteq V
as
V
is saturated.
But
\textbf{{s}}(G_{n})\subseteq V
only if
G_{n}=\varnothing
. Proceeding inductively, this implies that
G_{1}=\varnothing
which is a contradiction.

Now, since every
G_{n}
is non-empty, there is an infinite path
\mu=\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\cdots\in E^{\infty}
such that

\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n}\in G_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, ,
V\cap\{\textbf{{s}}(\mu_{k}):k\in\mathbb{N}\}\neq\varnothing
(a property of
V
) and

\textbf{{s}}(\mu_{k})\not\in V
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
(by definition of the
G_{n}
), a contradiction.
∎

5.6.
Local contractiveness

We now pass to discussing local contractivity of the inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
.
To this end we introduce one more condition that
(G,E)
may satisfy.

(Con)

Every vertex is reachable from a
G
-cycle with an entrance. That is, for any
v\in E^{0}
there is
\mu\in vE^{*}
such that
\textbf{{s}}(\mu)
is a base point of a
G
-cycle with an entrance.

Proposition 5.34
.

For any self-similar action
(G,E)
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

S(G,E)
is locally contracting;

(2)

S(G,E)
is strongly locally contracting; and

(3)

(G,E)
satisfies
(Con)
.

If any of the above equivalent conditions hold, then every
G
-cycle has an entrance.

Proof.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(2)
.
Let
\mu\in E^{*}
and put
v=\textbf{{s}}(\mu)
.
By assumption there are
\alpha\in vE^{*}
,
g\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)G
, and a
g
-cycle
\gamma\in\textbf{{r}}(g)E^{*}\textbf{{s}}(g)=\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}\textbf{{s}}(g)
with an entrance, so we may find
\gamma^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}
such that

\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are not comparable. Then
s\coloneqq(\mu\alpha\gamma,g^{-1},\mu\alpha)\in S(G,E)
. Putting
f_{1}\coloneqq f_{\mu\alpha}
,
f_{0}\coloneqq f_{\mu\alpha\gamma^{\prime}}

and using (
5.14
) we get

sf_{1}s^{*}=f_{\mu\alpha\gamma}\leq f_{1}
,
0\neq f_{0}\leq f_{1}=s^{*}s\leq f_{\mu}
and
f_{0}\cdot s=0
. Hence,
S(G,E)
is strongly locally contracting.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
. See Remark
3.16
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3)
.
Fix
v\in E^{0}
. Since
S(G,E)
is locally contractive, there exists
s=(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)
and
F=\{f_{0},\dots,f_{n}\}\subseteq f_{v}s^{*}s\mathcal{E}(G,E)\setminus\{0\}
such that
F
is a cover of
sf_{i}s^{*}
and
f_{0}(sf_{i}s^{*})=0
for all
0\leq i\leq n
.
Since
s^{*}s=f_{\beta}
,
f_{v}s^{*}s\neq 0
if and only if
\textbf{{r}}(\beta)=v
. The relation
f_{i}\in f_{v}s^{*}s\mathcal{E}(G,E)\setminus\{0\}
means that
f_{i}=f_{\beta\beta_{i}}

for some
\beta_{i}\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{*}
, and we have

sf_{i}s^{*}=sf_{\beta\beta_{i}}s^{*}=f_{\alpha g\beta_{i}}
by (
5.14
).
Hence, each
\alpha g\beta_{i}
is covered by
\{\beta\beta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and incomparable with
\beta\beta_{0}
.
In particular,
\beta
and
\alpha
are comparable, so
\textbf{{r}}(\alpha)=\textbf{{r}}(\beta)=v
. We consider three cases.

If
\alpha=\beta\beta^{\prime}
for some
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
, then
\beta^{\prime}
is a
g^{-1}
-cycle as
\textbf{{s}}(\beta^{\prime})=\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)=g\textbf{{s}}(\beta)=g\textbf{{r}}(\beta^{\prime})
.
Also, each
\beta^{\prime}g\beta_{i}
is incomparable with
\beta_{0}\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{*}=\textbf{{s}}(\beta^{\prime})E^{*}
.
By Lemma
5.20
, this cannot happen if
\beta^{\prime}
has no entrance.
Hence,
\beta^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{*}
is a
g^{-1}
-cycle with an entrance.

If
\beta=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
, then
\alpha^{\prime}
is a
g
-cycle as
\textbf{{r}}(\alpha^{\prime})=\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)=g\textbf{{s}}(\beta)=g\textbf{{s}}(\alpha^{\prime})
.
Also, each
g\beta_{i}\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}
is incomparable with
\alpha^{\prime}\beta_{0}
. By Lemma
5.20
this cannot happen if
\alpha^{\prime}
has no entrance.
Hence,
\alpha^{\prime}\in\textbf{{s}}(\alpha)E^{*}
is a
g
-cycle with an entrance.

If
\alpha=\beta
, then each
g\beta_{i}
is covered by
\{\beta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and incomparable with
\beta_{0}
.
This leads to a contradiction. Indeed, put
i_{0}=0
and for
k\geq 1
inductively choose
i_{k}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
\beta_{i_{k}}
is the shortest path amongst
\{\beta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
which is comparable with
g\beta_{k-1}
.
Since
\{\beta_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is finite there exist
k,l\geq 1
such that

\beta_{i_{k}}=\beta_{i_{k+l}}
. Assume
k
is the smallest with this property.
Note that
g\beta_{i_{k-1}}
and
g\beta_{i_{k+l-1}}
are comparable with
\beta_{i_{k}}
.
Since comparability of paths is transitive and preserved under
G
-action,
this means that
\beta_{i_{k-1}}
and
\beta_{i_{k+l-1}}
are comparable.
Since
\beta_{i_{0}}=\beta_{0}
is incomparable with
\beta_{i_{m}}
for all
m\geq 1
, we have
k-1\geq 1
.
Thus,
\beta_{i_{k-1}}
is not longer than
\beta_{i_{k+l-1}}
as it was chosen the shortest comparable with

g\beta_{i_{k-2}}
, and
\beta_{i_{k+l-1}}
is not longer than
\beta_{i_{k-1}}
as it is the shortest comparable with
g\beta_{i_{k+l-2}}
.
In other words,
\beta_{i_{k-1}}=\beta_{i_{k+l-1}}
.
But this contradicts minimality of
k
.

For the final assertion, the last part of Lemma
5.20

implies that every base point of a
G
-cycle without entrance fails to satisfy
(3)
.
∎

Remark 5.35
.

If every vertex is a range of a path whose source lies on a cycle (which holds, for example, when
E^{0}
is finite and there are no sources),
then the above equivalent conditions of Proposition
5.34
hold if and only if every cycle in
E
has an entrance.
This in particular gives
[
ExP17
]
*Theorem 15.1.

Example 5.36
.

Here we give an example of a self-similar group action for which
S(G,E)
is topologically free, minimal, and locally contracting, but not effective and (hence, necessarily) not closed.
Let
E
be the directed graph

v
f
e

and let
G=\mathbb{Z}
.
Identify
0\in\mathbb{Z}
with
v
and define a self-similar action of
\mathbb{Z}
on
E
, given on generators by

\displaystyle 1\cdot e

\displaystyle=e,

\displaystyle 1|_{e}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle 1\cdot f

\displaystyle=f,\text{ and}

\displaystyle 1|_{f}

\displaystyle=0.

Observe that each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
fixes every
\alpha\in E^{*}
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the path
e^{n}f
is a minimal strongly
k
-fixed path, so by Proposition
5.10
the inverse semigroup
S(\mathbb{Z},E)
is not closed. All (nontrivial) paths are cycles with an entrance, and each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
strongly fixes
f
, so
S(\mathbb{Z},E)
is topologically free by Proposition
5.23
. Since
E^{0}=\{v\}
there are no nontrivial
(\mathbb{Z},E)
-invariant sets of vertices, so by Propositions
5.33
and
5.30
the inverse semigroup
S(\mathbb{Z},E)
is minimal. Since
v
lies on a
\mathbb{Z}
-cycle with an entrance,
S(\mathbb{Z},E)
is locally contracting by Proposition
5.34
.

On the other hand,
S(\mathbb{Z},E)
does not satisfy condition
(Slack)
in Proposition
5.23
: each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
fixes
Z(v)
, but for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
the path
e^{n}
is not strongly
k
-fixed, so
k
is not slack at
v
.

6.
The groupoid analysis

In this section we analyse the basic properties (described in Definition

2.22
) of groupoids associated to a self-similar groupoid action
(G,E)
.

6.1.
Groupoids associated to self-similar actions

We start by describing the universal and tight groupoid (see Definition
1.15
) associated to the inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
.
The path space
E^{\leq\infty}
and boundary path space
\partial E
were introduced in Example
1.16
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
(G,E)
be a self-similar groupoid action. We have an action of the inverse semigroup
S(G,E)
on the path space
E^{\leq\infty}
of
E
, where for
(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)
the partial homeomorphisms

\widetilde{h}_{(\alpha,g,\beta)}\colon Z(\beta)\to Z(\alpha)
are given by

\widetilde{h}_{(\alpha,g,\beta)}(\beta\eta)=\alpha(g\cdot\eta),\qquad\eta\in E^{\leq\infty},\,\textbf{{r}}(\eta)=\textbf{{s}}(\beta).

The boundary path space
\partial E
is a closed
\widetilde{h}
-invariant subset of
E^{\leq\infty}
, and so
\widetilde{h}
restricts to an action
h
on
\partial E
.
In fact, the map
E^{\leq\infty}\ni\mu\mapsto\xi_{\mu}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}(G,E)}

given by
\xi_{\mu}\coloneqq\{(\alpha,\textbf{{s}}(\alpha),\alpha):\alpha\in E^{*},\,\mathbbm{1}_{Z(\alpha)}(\mu)=1\}
,
intertwines
\widetilde{h}
with the canonical action of
S(G,E)
on
\widehat{\mathcal{E}(G,E)}
, and restricts
to the homeomorphism
\partial E\cong\partial\widehat{\mathcal{E}(G,E)}
. Thus, we have natural isomorphisms

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(S(G,E))\cong S(G,E)\ltimes_{\widehat{h}}E^{\leq\infty}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{G}(S(G,E))\cong S(G,E)\ltimes_{h}\partial E

of topological groupoids.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{E}(G,E)\cong\mathcal{E}(E)
, it follows from Example
1.16
that the map

E^{\leq\infty}\ni\mu\mapsto\xi_{\mu}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}(G,E)}
where

\xi_{\mu}\coloneqq\{f_{\alpha}:\alpha\in E^{*},\,\mathbbm{1}_{Z(\alpha)}(\mu)=1\}

is a homeomorphism
E^{\leq\infty}\cong\widehat{\mathcal{E}(G,E)}
, which restricts to the homeomorphism
\partial E\cong\partial\widehat{\mathcal{E}(G,E)}
.
This homeomorphism intertwines the appropriate actions. Indeed, let
t=(\alpha,g,\beta)
. Then
\mu\in Z(\beta)

if and only if
f_{\beta}\in\xi_{\mu}
. Let

\mu=\beta\overline{\mu}\in Z(\beta)
for some
\overline{\mu}\in E^{\leq\infty}
. For the action
\widetilde{h}
on
E^{\leq\infty}
we have
\widetilde{h}_{t}(\mu)=\widetilde{h}_{(\alpha,g,\beta)}(\beta\overline{\mu})=\alpha(g\cdot\overline{\mu})
, and so
for
\eta\in E^{*}
we have

f_{\eta}\in\xi_{\widetilde{h}_{t}(\mu)}\Longleftrightarrow 1=\mathbbm{1}_{Z(\eta)}(\alpha(g\cdot\overline{\mu})).

On the other hand, using the same symbol
\widetilde{h}
for the canonical action on
\widehat{\mathcal{E}(G,E)}
,

\displaystyle f_{\eta}\in\widetilde{h}_{t}(\xi_{\mu})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inverse_semigroup_action_spectrum}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}t^{*}f_{\eta}t\in\xi_{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:pre_fixed_characterisation}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}(f_{\beta(g^{-1}\alpha^{\prime})}\in\xi_{\mu}\text{ and }\eta=\alpha\alpha^{\prime})\text{ or }(f_{\beta}\in\xi_{\mu}\text{ and }\alpha=\eta\eta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu=\beta\overline{\mu}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}1=\mathbbm{1}_{Z(g^{-1}\alpha^{\prime})}(\overline{\mu})\text{ and }\eta=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\text{ or }\alpha=\eta\eta^{\prime}.

Now we have three cases. If
\eta
and
\alpha
are not comparable, then the above conditions imply that
f_{\eta}\not\in\xi_{\widetilde{h}_{t}(\mu)}
and
f_{\eta}\not\in\widetilde{h}_{t}(\xi_{\mu})
.
If
\alpha=\eta\eta^{\prime}
, then
f_{\eta}\in\xi_{\widetilde{h}_{t}(\mu)}
and
f_{\eta}\in\widetilde{h}_{t}(\xi_{\mu})
.
If
\eta=\alpha\alpha^{\prime}
, then

\mathbbm{1}_{Z(\eta)}(\alpha(g\cdot\overline{\mu}))=\mathbbm{1}_{Z(\alpha^{\prime})}(g\cdot\overline{\mu})=\mathbbm{1}_{Z(g^{-1}\alpha^{\prime})}(\overline{\mu}),

and so
f_{\eta}\in\xi_{\widetilde{h}_{t}(\mu)}
if and only if
f_{\eta}\in\widetilde{h}_{t}(\xi_{\mu})
. Thus,
\widetilde{h}_{t}(\xi_{\mu})=\xi_{\widetilde{h}_{t}(\mu)}
as claimed.
This implies the remaining assertions.
∎

To describe, somewhat more concretely, the above transformation groupoids we consider the following equivalence relation on the set of quadruples
(\alpha,g,\beta;\xi)
, where
(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)\text{ and }\xi\in Z(\beta)
:

(\alpha,g,\beta;\xi)\sim(\gamma,h,\delta;\eta)\iff\begin{cases}(\alpha,g,\beta)\text{ and }(\gamma,h,\delta)\text{ share a lower bound }(\epsilon,k,\zeta)\\
\text{ and }\xi=\eta\in Z(\zeta)\end{cases}

Denote the equivalence class of
(\alpha,g,\beta;\xi)
by
[\alpha,g,\beta;\xi]
.
We equip

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)\coloneqq\{[\alpha,g,\beta;\beta\xi]\colon(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)\text{ and }\xi\in Z(\textbf{{s}}(\beta))\}

with the structure of an étale groupoid with multiplication given by

[\alpha,g,\beta;\beta\xi]\cdot[\gamma,h,\delta;\delta\eta]\coloneqq[(\alpha,g,\beta)\cdot(\gamma,h,\delta);\delta\eta]\qquad\text{whenever $\beta\xi=\gamma(h\cdot\eta)$,}

inverse
[\alpha,g,\beta;\beta\xi]^{-1}=[\beta,g^{-1},\alpha;\alpha(g\cdot\xi)]
,
and a basis for the topology given by the bisections

U(\alpha,g,\beta;V)\coloneqq\{[\alpha,g,\beta;\xi]\mid\xi\in V\}

where
V\subseteq Z(\beta)
is open.
The unit space

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)^{0}=\{[\textbf{{r}}(\xi),\textbf{{r}}(\xi),\textbf{{r}}(\xi);\xi]\colon\xi\in E^{\leq\infty}\}

is naturally homeomorphic to
E^{\leq\infty}
, and we use this homeomorphism to identify
\mathcal{G}(G,E)^{0}
with
E^{\leq\infty}
.
Then the source and range maps are given by

(6.2)

\textbf{{s}}([\alpha,g,\beta;\beta\xi])=\beta\xi,\qquad\textbf{{r}}([\alpha,g,\beta;\beta\xi])=\alpha(g\cdot\xi).

The boundary path space
\partial E
is a closed
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
-invariant subset of
E^{\leq\infty}
and hence

\mathcal{G}(G,E)\coloneqq\{[\alpha,g,\beta;\xi]\colon(\alpha,g,\beta)\in S(G,E)\text{ and }\xi\in Z(\beta)\cap\partial E\}

is a closed full subgroupoid of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
.
By construction these groupoids are isomorphic to the transformation groupoids described in Proposition
6.1
, and so
we have natural groupoid isomorphisms

(6.3)

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(S(G,E))\qquad\mathcal{G}(G,E)\cong\mathcal{G}(S(G,E)).

These groupoids are
\mathbb{Z}
-graded by the continuous groupoid homomorphism
c:\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)\to\mathbb{Z}

given by
c([\alpha,g,\beta;\xi])=|\alpha|-|\beta|.

We identify the two clopen subgroupoids

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{0}(G,E)

\displaystyle\coloneqq c^{-1}(0)=\{[\alpha,g,\beta;\xi]\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)\colon|\alpha|=|\beta|\}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E),\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{0}(G,E)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{0}(G,E)\cap\mathcal{G}(G,E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}(G,E).

Here, we again have natural groupoid isomorphisms

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{0}(G,E)\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(S_{0}(G,E)),\qquad\mathcal{G}_{0}(G,E)\cong\mathcal{G}(S_{0}(G,E)),

where
S_{0}(G,E)
is the core subsemigroup of
S(G,E)
, given by (
5.4
).
Since
S_{00}(G,E)\cong G*E^{*}\cup\{0\}
is a wide inverse subsemigroup of
S_{0}(G,E)
, by the last part of Remark
1.10
,
we also get the following open subgroupoids

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{00}(G,E)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{[g\beta,g|_{\beta},\beta;\beta\xi]:(g,\beta)\in G*E^{*},\,\xi\in\textbf{{s}}(\beta)E^{\leq\infty}\}\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(S_{00}(G,E)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{00}(G,E)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{00}(G,E)\cap\mathcal{G}(G,E)\cong\mathcal{G}(S_{00}(G,E))

of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{0}(G,E)
and
\mathcal{G}_{0}(G,E)
respectively.
Thus, the discussed groupoids form the diagram:

(6.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{0}(G,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{00}(G,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(G,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{0}(G,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{00}(G,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\supseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

where the horizontal inclusions describe open subgroupoids while the vertical ones are closed subgroupoid inclusions.
We may also use that the inverse semigroups
\mathcal{E}(E)\subseteq S(E)_{0}\subseteq S(E)
sit naturally as wide subgroups in

S(G,E)
to complement the diagram (
6.4
) with the following diagrams of inclusions of wide open subgroupoids:

(6.5)
