reduced Toeplitz
, the
essential Toeplitz
,
the
reduced
and the
essential
L^{P}
-operator algebra
of
(G,E,\sigma)
, respectively.
In addition, using the notation from Corollary
9.10
and Definition
9.6
, we define for
*=0,00
the following
core subalgebras

\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\coloneqq B(W^{P},T^{P})_{*},\qquad\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\coloneqq B(\widetilde{W}^{P},\widetilde{T}^{P})_{*},

\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\coloneqq B(W^{\mathrm{r},P},T^{\mathrm{r},P})_{*},\qquad\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\coloneqq B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{r},P},\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{r},P})_{*},

\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\coloneqq B(W^{\mathrm{e},P},T^{\mathrm{e},P})_{*},\qquad\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\coloneqq B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{e},P},\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{e},P})_{*}.

Remark 9.12
.

By Corollary
A.6
, for each
*=\textup{\mbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}\vbox{\hrule width=5.0pt}\hbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}}\,,\mathrm{red},\mathrm{ess}
, the algebras

\mathcal{O}^{P}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)
are equipped with gauge circle actions coming from the length cocycle
(
5.3
). Subalgebras
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)_{0}
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)_{0}
are fixed-point
algebras for these actions.
The smaller core subalgebras
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)_{00}
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)_{00}
are generated by diagonal subalgebras and
representations of
(G,\sigma_{G})
. They carry crucial information about Hausdorffness and amenability of the underlying groupoids.

Remark 9.13
.

By Lemma
A.2
, for each
*=0,00
the core subalgebras
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
are reduced Banach algebras
of

(\mathcal{G}_{*}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
and
(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{*}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
, respectively.
Similarly,

\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
are essential Banach algebras
of

(\mathcal{G}_{*}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
and
(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{*}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
, respectively.

Remark 9.14
.

By
[
BKM25
]
*Theorem 5.13, see Remark
2.10
, if
P\subseteq\{1,\infty\}
, then
for any
*=\textup{\mbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}\vbox{\hrule width=5.0pt}\hbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}},0,00
, we have

\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}=\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}=\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}.

In particular, the same holds if
\{1,\infty\}\subseteq P
as then the associated
L^{P}
-operator algebras coincide with
L^{\{1,\infty\}}
-operator algebras. If the corresponding groupoid is amenable,
we have
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)_{*}=\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)_{*}
or
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E)_{*}=\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)_{*}
for every
P\subseteq[1,\infty]
, see Remark
2.11
.

Lemma 9.15
.

For any
\varnothing\neq P\subseteq[1,\infty]
we have natural injective homomorphisms, which are isometric into the full and reduced algebras,

\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}(E)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma),\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma),\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma),

\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}(E)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma),\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma),\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma).

Proof.

We consider the case
P=\{p\}
. One gets general case by considering direct sums of representations.
We use the embeddings

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
and
\mathcal{G}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{G}(G,E)
from (
6.5
).
The compression of the action of the algebra
B(\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{r},p})
to the (invariant) subspace
\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E))\subseteq\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E))

coincides with the canonical representation of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}^{p}(S(E))=F_{\mathrm{red}}^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E))
. Since the groupoid
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E)
is amenable,
we have
F_{\mathrm{red}}^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E))=F^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E))
by
[
GaL17
]
*Theorem 6.19. Thus,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}^{p}(S(E))=F^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E))=\mathcal{T}^{p}(S(E))=\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)
.
This implies that the canonical maps
\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)\to B(\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{r},p})\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}^{p}(S(E))
are isometric. Similar arguments give
\mathcal{O}^{p}(E)\cong B(T^{\mathrm{r},p})
.

Now composing the inclusion
\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)\cong F^{p}_{\mathrm{red}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
with the canonical homomorphism
\mathcal{T}^{p}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)\to\mathcal{T}^{p}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)\cong F^{p}_{\mathrm{ess}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
we get a contractive homomorphism
\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)\to\mathcal{T}^{p}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
that intertwines the canonical expectations
\mathcal{T}^{p}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)\to\mathcal{D}(E^{\leq\infty})
and

\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)\to C_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(E^{\leq\infty})
, cf. Remark
2.19
. Since these expectations are faithful we conclude that

\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)\to\mathcal{T}^{p}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
is injective.
Similar arguments give
\mathcal{O}^{p}(E)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{p}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
.
Since
\mathcal{O}^{p}(E)
and
\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)
contain
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)

and
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})
as subalgebras, this finishes the proof.
∎

To say something about the canonical representation of
(G,\sigma_{G})
in the above algebras
we need more terminology.
Suppose that
G
acts self-similarly on
E
. Since the left action of
g\in G
establishes a bijection
\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}\cong\textbf{{r}}(g)E^{1}

we see that
\textbf{{s}}(g)
is a source or infinite receiver if and only if
\textbf{{r}}(g)
has this property.
In particular, the complementary sets
E^{0}_{\reg}
and
E^{0}_{\sing}
are
G
-invariant and
G=G_{\reg}\sqcup G_{\sing}
decomposes into a disjoint union
of the corresponding restrictions

G_{\reg}\coloneqq G|_{E^{0}_{\reg}}=\{g\in G\mid\textbf{{r}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g)\in E^{0}_{\reg}\},\quad G_{\sing}\coloneqq G|_{E^{0}_{\sing}}=\{g\in G\mid\textbf{{r}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g)\in E^{0}_{\sing}\}

is a subgroupoid of
G
.
We let
K_{0}\coloneqq E^{0}
and recursively define

K_{n+1}\coloneqq E^{0}_{\sing}\sqcup\{g\in G_{\reg}:\text{$ge=e$ and $g|_{e}\in K_{n}$ for each $e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}$}\},\qquad n\geq 0.

Then
K_{0}\subseteq K_{1}\subseteq K_{2}\subseteq\cdots
and
K\coloneqq\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}K_{n}

is the smallest wide subgroupoid of
G
such that for any
g\in G_{\reg}

(9.16)

\text{$ge=e$ and $g|_{e}\in K$ for all $e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}$ }\,\Longrightarrow\,\,\,g\in K.

This groupoid
K
is an algebraic analogue of a construction of a reduction ideal for
C^{*}
-correspondences, see

[
KwL13
]
*Definition 5.2. We follow the naming of Miller and Steinberg who called
K
the tight kernel, see
[
MiS
]
*Subsection 2.3.

Definition 9.17

(
[
MiS
]
)
.

We call
K
constructed above the
tight kernel
of
(G,E)
. Explicitly,

K=\{g\in G_{\reg}:\exists_{n\geq 1}\text{ $g$ strongly fixes all paths in $\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{n}$ and in $\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{k}_{\sing}$ for $k\leq n$}\}.

Remark 9.18
.

It is immediate that
K
is a normal subgroupoid of
G
and that elements in
K
fix all paths in
E^{*}
(as extension of strongly fixed paths are strongly fixed). Thus,
K
is a subgroupoid of the kernel
N
of the action of
G
, cf. Remark
4.15
. By (
9.16
),
K
is closed under taking sections.
Hence, the quotient
G/K
groupoid acts self-similarily on
E
by the formulas

(gK)\mu=g\mu,\qquad(gK)|_{\mu}=g|_{\mu}K,\qquad(g,\mu)\in G*E^{*}.

By
[
MiS
]
*Theorem 2.12, the surjective semigroup homomorphism
S(G,E)\ni(\alpha,g,\beta)\mapsto(\alpha,gK,\beta)\in S(G/K,E)
induces
isomorphism of tight groupoids

\mathcal{G}(G,E)\cong\mathcal{G}(G/K,E).

Lemma 9.19
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{G}
is a twist of
G
which is invariant under sections, equivalently
\sigma\coloneqq(\sigma_{G},1)
is
a twist of
(G,E)
, cf Remark
8.3
. Then
\sigma/K\coloneqq(\sigma_{G/K},1)
, where
\sigma_{G/K}(gK,hK)\coloneqq\sigma_{G}(g,h)
for
(g,h)\in G^{2}
,
is a well-defined twist of
(G/K,E)
and

(\mathcal{G}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})\cong(\mathcal{G}(G/K,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma/K}).

Moreover, for every covariant representation
(W,T)
of
(G,E,\sigma)
,
the
\sigma_{G}
-twisted representation
G\ni g\mapsto W_{g}
of
G
descends to
a
\sigma/K
-twisted representation

G/K\ni gK\mapsto W_{g}
of
G/K
.

Proof.

Let
(g,h,k)\in G^{2}*K
. Find
\mu
which is strongly fixed by
k
. Then using that
\sigma_{G}
is invariant under sections
we get

\sigma_{G}(g,hk)=\sigma_{G}(g|_{hk\mu},hk|_{\mu})=\sigma_{G}(g|_{h},h|_{\mu})=\sigma_{G}(g,k).

Similarly, for
(g,k,h)\in G*K*G
finding
\mu
which is (strongly) fixed by
k
we get

\sigma_{G}(gk,h)=\sigma_{G}(gk|_{\mu},h|_{h^{-1}\mu})=\sigma_{G}(g|_{\mu},h|_{h^{-1}\mu})=\sigma_{G}(g,h).

Hence,
\sigma_{G/K}
is well defined. Clearly, it is an invariant under sections
2
-cocycle for
G/K
.
Thus,
\sigma/K\coloneqq(\sigma_{G/K},1)
is a twist of
(G/K,E)
. It is immediate that
the associated twists
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma/K}
are isomorphic through the isomorphism respecting
\mathcal{G}(G,E)\cong\mathcal{G}(G/K,E)
.

For the second part of the assertion it suffices to show that
W_{k}=W_{\textbf{{s}}(k)}
for all
k\in K
.
This is trivial when
k\in K_{0}
. Assume this holds for all elements in
K_{n}
and let
k\in K_{n+1}
.
Thus,
k\in G_{\reg}
and
ke=e
,
k|_{e}\in K_{n}
for each
e\in\textbf{{s}}(k)E^{1}
.
Using this we get

W_{k}=W_{k}W_{\textbf{{s}}(k)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{defn:Cuntz--Krieger2}}}{{=}}W_{k}\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(k)E^{1}}T_{e}T_{e}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{enu:reps_of_twisted_self_similar3}}}{{=}}\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(k)E^{1}}T_{ke}W_{k|_{e}}T_{e}^{*}=\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(k)E^{1}}T_{e}T_{e}^{*}=W_{\textbf{{s}}(k)}.

Hence, the assertion holds by induction.
∎

Definition 9.20
.

We say that
(G,E)
is
tightly faithful
if
K=G^{0}
, equivalently there is no
g\in G_{\reg}\setminus G^{0}
that strongly fixes all elements in
\textbf{{s}}(g)\partial E
.

Remark 9.21
.

If
(G,E)
is faithful or pseudo free then it is tightly faithful, but in general all three properties are different.
The authors of
[
MiS
]
say that an action is
loosely faithful
if
K=N
.
Hence, an action is faithful if and only if it is both tightly and loosely faithful.

Proposition 9.22
.

Let
(G,E,\sigma)
be a twisted self-similar action and let
\varnothing\neq P\subseteq[1,\infty]
.

(1)

The subalgebra
B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{r},P})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
is an exotic algebra of
(G,\sigma_{G})
, in the sense that we have a canonical representation

B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{r},P})\to F^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

(2)

If
E
is row-finite, then the subalgebra
B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{e},P})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
is an exotic algebra of
(G,\sigma_{G})
in the sense that we have a canonical representation

B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{e},P})\to F^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

(3)

Assume
\sigma_{\bowtie}\equiv 1
is trivial. Then
the
\sigma_{G}
-twisted representation
G\ni g\mapsto W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{g}\in\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
is injective if and only if
(G,E)
is tightly faithful.
In general, it descends to an injective representation

G/K\ni gK\mapsto W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{g}\in\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)

of
(G/K,\sigma/K)
.

(4)

If
(G,E)
is pseudo free, then
B(W^{\mathrm{r},P})
is exotic in the sense that we have a canonical representation

B(W^{\mathrm{r},P})\to F^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

Proof.

(1)
.
Note that the closed subgroupoid

(9.23)

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G)\coloneqq\{[\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g);\textbf{{s}}(g)]:g\in G\}

of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
is isomorphic to
G
, and the subspace
\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G))\subseteq\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E))
is invariant under the action of
B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{r},p})
. Moreover, restricting this action to
\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G))\cong\ell^{p}(G)
, we get a map
G\in g\mapsto\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{r},p}_{g}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G)))
which is equivalent to the
regular representation of
(G,\sigma)
. This gives a natural representation
B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{r},p})\to F^{p}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

(2)
. If
E
is row-finite (there are no infinite receivers), than
every point in the subgroupoid
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G)
, given by (
9.23
), is open in
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
. Hence,
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)_{\Hau}
, as the latter is comeager in
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
.
As in
(1)
, the supspace
\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G))\subseteq\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)_{\Hau})
is invariant under the action of
B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{e},p})
,
and the restriction to
\ell^{p}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G))\cong\ell^{p}(G)
gives representation
B(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{e},p})\to F^{p}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma)
.

(3)
. By Lemma
9.19
,
W^{\mathrm{r},P}
descends to
G/K
and so it cannot be injective
if
K
is nontrivial. So assume
(G,E)
is tightly faithful and let
g\in G\setminus G^{0}
.
If
g\not\in G_{\reg}
, that is
\textbf{{s}}(g)\in E^{0}_{\sing}
, then
\gamma\coloneqq[\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g)]
is an element of
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
different than
[\textbf{{s}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g)]
which we identify with
\textbf{{s}}(g)\in\partial E
. Since

W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{g}\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}=\mathbbm{1}_{\gamma}\neq\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}=W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}
, we get that
W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{g}\neq W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}
.
If
\gamma\in G_{\reg}
, then by tight faithfulness, there is
\mu\in\textbf{{s}}(g)\partial E
, which is not strongly
g
-fixed. Then
\gamma\coloneqq[\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g),\mu]
and

\eta\coloneqq[\textbf{{s}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g),\textbf{{s}}(g),\mu]
are different elements of
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
. Since

W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{g}\mathbbm{1}_{\eta}=\mathbbm{1}_{\gamma}\neq\mathbbm{1}_{\eta}=W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}\mathbbm{1}_{\eta}
, we get that
W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{g}\neq W^{\mathrm{r},P}_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}
.
Hence,
W^{\mathrm{r},P}
is an injective representation of
(G,\sigma_{G})
.

(4)
.
Consider
f=\sum_{g\in F}\alpha_{g}\mathbbm{1}_{g}\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
and

\xi=\sum_{h\in H}\beta_{h}\mathbbm{1}_{h}\in\ell^{p}(G)
where
F,H\subseteq G
are finite and
\alpha_{g}
’s and
\beta_{h}
’s are complex numbers.
For each
v\in E^{0}=G^{0}
choose any
\xi_{v}\in v\partial E
.
By pseudo freeness, see Proposition
7.2
(3)
,
we have
[(\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g),\xi]=[(\textbf{{r}}(h),h,\textbf{{s}}(h),\xi^{\prime}]
if and only if
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
and
g=h
. Hence, the map
H\ni h\mapsto[\textbf{{r}}(h),h,\textbf{{s}}(h);\xi_{\textbf{{s}}(h)}]\in\mathcal{G}(G,E)

is injective, so for
\xi^{\prime}=\sum_{h\in H}\beta_{h}\mathbbm{1}_{[\textbf{{r}}(h),h,\textbf{{s}}(h);\xi_{\textbf{{s}}(h)}]}\in\ell^{p}(\mathcal{G}(G,E))
we have

\|\xi\|_{p}=\|\xi^{\prime}\|_{p}
, and also

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{p}(f)\xi\|_{p}

\displaystyle=\|f*\xi\|_{p}=\begin{cases}\big(\sum_{k\in G}|\sum_{gh=k}\sigma_{G}(g,h)\alpha_{g}\beta_{h}|^{p}\big)^{1/p},&p<\infty\\
\sup_{k\in G}|\sum_{gh=k}\sigma_{G}(g,h)\alpha_{g}\beta_{h}|,&p=\infty\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{k\in G}\sum_{gh=k}\sigma_{G}(g,h)\alpha_{g}\beta_{h}\mathbbm{1}_{[\textbf{{r}}(k),k,\textbf{{s}}(k);\xi_{\textbf{{s}}(k)}]}\right\|_{p}=\left\|\sum_{g\in F}\alpha_{g}W_{g}^{\mathrm{r},p}\xi^{\prime}\right\|_{p}.

This implies that
\|\Lambda_{p}(f)\|_{\mathrm{red}}^{p}\leq\|\sum_{g\in F}\alpha_{g}W_{g}^{\mathrm{r},p}\|
. Hence,
the map
B(W^{\mathrm{r},p})\ni\sum_{g\in F}\alpha_{g}W_{g}^{\mathrm{r},p}\mapsto\sum_{g\in F}\alpha_{g}\mathbbm{1}_{g}\in F^{p}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
is well-defined and contractive.
∎

Remark 9.24
.

Without the row-finiteness assumption item
(2)
above fails, see Remark
10.22
below.
Pseudo freeness in
(4)
seems too strong, while tight faithfulness in
(3)
seems too weak
to show that
B(W^{\mathrm{r},P})
is an exotic algebra of
(G,\sigma_{G})
. For amenable actions with tight faithfulness we could prove that
the
C^{*}
-algebraic singular ideal vanishes, in the same manner as in Corollary
10.21
below. Thus, we have the following open problem, which is already important in the
C^{*}
-algebraic case.

Problem 9.25
.

For which twisted self-similar actions
(G,E,\sigma)
is the closure of an image of
\textup{C}_{c}(G,\sigma_{G})

in
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)
an exotic Banach algebra of
(G,\sigma_{G})
?

9.3.
Main structural results

The diagonal subalgebras
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)
and
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})
in Remark
9.24
, both contain a copy of the algebra
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
. Namely, it is the closed linear span of characteristic functions
\{\mathbbm{1}_{Z(v)\cap\partial E}\}_{v\in E^{0}}
and
\{\mathbbm{1}_{Z(v)}\}_{v\in E^{0}}
, respectively.
The algebra
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
plays a crucial role in “uniqueness theorem” for Cuntz algebras. For Toeplitz algebras we instead need to consider the larger
subalgebra

\clsp\{\mathbbm{1}_{Z(\alpha)}:\alpha\in E^{0}\cup E^{1}\}

of
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})
. Its spectrum can be naturally identified with the discrete space
E^{\leq 1}\coloneqq E^{0}\cup E^{1}
, so we denote this subalgebra by
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq 1})
.

Lemma 9.26
.

Every representation
\psi:\mathcal{O}^{P}(E)\to B
that is nonzero on each projection corresponding to vertices in
E^{0}
(is injective on
C_{0}(E^{0})
) is isometric on
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)
.

Proof.

Let
(W,T)
be the Cuntz–Krieger
E
-family generating
\mathcal{O}^{P}(E)
. Put
V_{\mu}\coloneqq\psi(T_{\mu}T_{\mu}^{*})
for
\mu\in E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
and

V_{v}\coloneqq\psi(W_{v})
for
v\in E^{0}
. By assumption
V_{v}\neq 0
for every
v\in E^{0}
.
Since
\psi(T_{e}T_{e}^{*})=\psi(W_{\textbf{{r}}(e)})\neq 0
we have
\psi(T_{e})\neq 0
.
If
(e,f)\in E^{2}
, then the
E
-family relations imply that
T_{e}^{*}(T_{ef}T_{ef}^{*})T_{f}=W_{\textbf{{r}}(f)}
.
This implies that
\psi(T_{ef})\neq 0
. Proceeding inductively one concludes that
\psi(T_{\mu})\neq 0
and so also
V_{\mu}\neq 0
for every
\mu\in E^{*}
.
By the last part of Proposition
3.9

we need to show that
\prod_{\beta\in F}(V_{\mu}-V_{\beta})\neq 0
for every
\mu\in E^{*}
and finite
F\subseteq\mu E^{*}
that does not cover
\mu
in the semigroup
E^{*}\cup 0
.
As explained in Example
1.16
, the latter means that there is an extension
\alpha
of
\mu
which is not comparable with any path in
F
.
Assuming this,
E
-family relations imply that
V_{\alpha}\cdot V_{\mu}=V_{\alpha}\neq 0
and

V_{\alpha}\cdot V_{\beta}=0
for all
\beta\in F
.
Hence,
V_{\alpha}\cdot\prod_{\beta\in F}(V_{\mu}-V_{\beta})=V_{\alpha}\neq 0
, and so
\prod_{\beta\in F}(V_{\mu}-V_{\beta})\neq 0
.
∎

The following proposition for
C^{*}
-algebras was proved in
[
FoR99
]
*Theorem 4.1 using
C^{*}
-correspondence techniques (cf. also
[
CKO19
]
*Theorem 9.1).
We use groupoid models.

Proposition 9.27
.

For any representation
\psi:\mathcal{T}^{P}(E)\to B
the following are equivalent:

(1)

\psi
is injective on
\mathcal{T}^{P}(E)
;

(2)

\psi
is injective on
C_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})
;

(3)

\psi
is injective on the algebra
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq 1})=\clsp\{\mathbbm{1}_{Z(\alpha)}:\alpha\in E^{0}\cup E^{1}\}\subseteq C_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})
;

(4)

the representation
\psi
satisfies the following condition

(9.28)

\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{Z(v)})\neq\sum_{e\in\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)}\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{Z(e)})\text{ for all }v\in E^{0}_{\reg}\text{ and }\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{Z(v)})\neq 0\text{ for all }v\in E^{0}_{\sing}.

In particular, for a representation
(W,T)
of
E
on an
L^{p}
-space
Y
the associated representation
\mathcal{T}^{p}(E)\to\mathbb{B}(Y)
is injective if and only if

W_{v}\neq 0
for all
v\in E^{0}
and

W_{v}\neq\sum_{e\in\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)}T_{e}T_{e}^{*}\qquad\text{ for all }v\in E^{0}_{\reg}.

Proof.

Implications

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
\Rightarrow
(3)
\Rightarrow
(4)
are obvious. Also since
\mathcal{T}^{P}(E)\cong F^{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E))
, by Theorem
9.7
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(E)
is topologically free, by Theorem
6.24
(3)
, the implication
(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
holds by
Theorem
2.23
(2)
.

Thus, it suffices to prove that
(4)
\Rightarrow
(1)
.
Assume (
9.28
). Denote by
(W,T)
the
E
-family generating
\mathcal{T}^{P}(E)
, and put
V_{\mu}\coloneqq\psi(T_{\mu}T_{\mu}^{*})
for
\mu\in E^{*}\setminus E^{0}
, and

V_{v}\coloneqq\psi(W_{v})=\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{Z(v)})
for
v\in E^{0}
. By (
9.28
),
V_{v}\neq 0
for all
v\in E^{0}

and
as in the proof Lemma
9.26
we get that
V_{\mu}\neq 0
for all
\mu\in E^{*}
.
By the last part of Proposition
3.9
,
\psi
is isometric on
C_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})

if and only if
\prod_{\beta\in F}(V_{\mu}-V_{\beta})\neq 0
for every
\mu\in E^{*}
and every finite
F\subseteq\mu E^{*}\setminus\{\mu\}
.
This condition readily implies that
\psi(W_{v})>\sum_{e\in\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)}\psi(T_{e}T_{e}^{*})
for any
v\in E^{0}_{\reg}
(take
\mu=v
and
F=\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)
).
Conversely, suppose that
\psi(W_{v})>\sum_{e\in\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)}\psi(T_{e}T_{e}^{*})
for every
v\in E^{0}_{\reg}
.
Take any
\mu\in E^{*}
and any finite
F\subseteq\mu E^{*}\setminus\{\mu\}
.
If
v\coloneqq\textbf{{s}}(\mu)
is a source, then
F
has to be empty. If
v
is an infinite receiver, then there is an edge
e\in\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)
which is not a prefix of any path in
F
.
Then
V_{\mu e}\cdot V_{\mu}=V_{\mu e}\neq 0
and

V_{\mu e}\cdot V_{\beta}=0
for all
\beta\in F
which implies that
\prod_{\beta\in F}(V_{\mu}-V_{\beta})
is nonzero (it contains
V_{\mu e}
as a subprojection).
If
v=\textbf{{s}}(\mu)\in E^{0}_{\reg}
is regular, then by the assumption
V_{v}>\sum_{e\in\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)}V_{e}
.
Since
\phi(T_{\mu}^{*})V_{\mu}\phi(T_{\mu}^{*})=V_{v}
and
\phi(T_{\mu}^{*})V_{\mu e}\phi(T_{\mu}^{*})=V_{e}
, this implies that

V_{\mu}-\sum_{e\in\textbf{{r}}^{-1}(v)}V_{\mu e}\neq 0
. This latter (nonzero) projection is a subprojection of
\prod_{\beta\in F}(V_{\mu}-V_{\beta})
.
∎

Definition 9.29

(cf.
[
BaK24
]
*Definition 5.6)
.

We say that an inclusion
A\subseteq B
of Banach algebras has the
intersection property
, or that
A

detects ideals
in
B
, if for every nonzero ideal
J
in
B
we have
J\cap A\neq\{0\}
.

Remark 9.30
.

An inclusion
A\subseteq B
has the intersection property if and only if every representation
\psi
of
B
which is injective on
A
, is injective on
B
.
Results assuring the latter are often called “uniqueness theorems”.
Proposition
9.27
says that

\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq 1})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}(E)
has the intersection property.
Lemma
9.26
implies that
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}(E)
has the intersection property if and only if
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}(E)
has the intersection property.

Theorem 9.31

(Intersection properties)
.

Let
(G,E)
be a self-similar groupoid action with a twist
\sigma
and let
P\subseteq[1,\infty]
be a non-empty set.

(1)

(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
imply that every ideal
I
in
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)
with
I\cap\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})=\{0\}
is contained in the kernel of the canonical map
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)\to\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
.
The converse implication holds when the twist is trivial.

(2)

(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
imply that
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
has the intersection property.
If
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)
has the intersection property, then
(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
hold.

(3)

(Evr)
and
(Rec)
imply that every kernel of a representation
\psi
of
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)
satisfying (
9.28
) is contained in the kernel of the canonical map
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)\to\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
.
The converse implication holds when the twist is trivial.

(4)

(Evr)
and
(Rec)
imply that
C_{0}(E^{\leq 1})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
has the intersection property.
If
C_{0}(E^{\leq 1})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E)
has the intersection property, then
(Evr)
and
(Rec)
hold.

(5)

(Evr)
implies that the two inclusions

\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{0},\,\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{00}

have the intersection property.

(6)

(Evr)
and
(Rec)
imply that
the inclusions
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
have the intersection property for all
*=\textup{\mbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}\vbox{\hrule width=5.11108pt}\hbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}},0,00
.

Proof.

(1)
.
Recall that
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)\cong F^{P}(\mathcal{G}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
,
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)\cong F^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(\mathcal{G}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
and
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
is topologically free if and only if

(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
hold, by Theorem
6.24
.
By Lemmas
9.26
,
9.15
, an ideal
I
in
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)
satisfies
I\cap\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})=\{0\}

if and only if
I\cap\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)=\{0\}
. We get the assertion by Theorem
2.23
(1)
.

(2)
. We get the assertion in a similar way as in
(2)
but we need to appeal to Theorem
2.23
(2)
. In particular, by Lemma
9.26
,
9.15
,

\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
has the intersection property
if and only if
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
has the intersection property.

(3)
. Recall that
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)\cong F^{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ess}}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)\cong F^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
is topologically free if and only if

(Evr)
and
(Rec)
hold, by Theorem
6.24
.
Let
I
be a kernel of a representation
\psi
of
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)
(every ideal in
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)
is of this form).
By Lemmas
9.26
,
9.15
,

I\cap\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq 1})=\{0\}
if and only if
I\cap\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})=\{0\}
.
Thus
we get the assertion by Theorem
2.23
(1)
.

(4)
. The argument in
(3)
and Theorem
2.23
(2)

gives the claim.

(5)
. Let
*=0,00
.
By Theorem
6.24
, topological freeness of
\mathcal{G}_{*}(G,E)

is equivalent to
(Evr)
.
By Remark
9.13
,
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
is an essential Banach algebra of the twisted groupoid

(\mathcal{G}_{*}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
.
Hence, the assertion follows from
[
BKM
]
*Theorem 5.10(2).

(6)
. Let
*=0,00
. By Theorem
6.24
, topological freeness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{*}(G,E)

is equivalent to
(Evr)
and
(Rec)
.
By Remark
9.13
,
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
is an essential Banach algebras of the twisted groupoid

(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{*}(G,E),\mathcal{L}_{\sigma})
.
Hence, the assertion follows from
[
BKM
]
*Theorem 5.10(2).
∎

Corollary 9.32
.

Theorem
H
in the introduction holds.

Proof.

Theorem
9.31
(1)
implies that

\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)
has the intersection property if and only if the map
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)\to\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E)
is injective
and both
(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
hold. Similarly, Theorem
9.31
(3)
gives that

\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq 1})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E)
has the intersection property if and only if the map
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E)\to\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E)
is injective
and both
(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
hold.

By the assumption in Theorem
H
and Corollary
6.21
, both groupoids
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)

are finitely non-Hausdorff. Hence, by Theorem
A
, injectivity of
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)\to\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E)
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)\to\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E)

is equivalent to
(Hum)
for
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
, respectively.

Amenability of
\mathcal{G}_{00}(G,E)
or
G
is equivalent to amenability of
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
, respectively (see
[
MiS
]
*Theorem 2.18 and Theorem
10.18
below).
Assuming this we get that
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)=\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}(G,E)=\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)
, respectively, see Remark
9.14
.

All this holds independently of the choice of
P
. Hence the assertion follows.
∎

In the setting of
C^{*}
-algebras a Cartan inclusion is a structure consisting of an algebra
B
together with a maximal abelian subalgebra
A\subseteq B
equipped with a faithful contractive projection
E:B\twoheadrightarrow A
.
We now describe analogous structures for
L^{P}
-algebras associated to self-similar actions.
We recall that the symbol

stands for the empty space.

Theorem 9.33
.

Let
(G,E,\sigma)
be a twisted self-similar groupoid action. Let
\varnothing\neq P\subseteq[1,\infty]
and
*=\textup{\mbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}\vbox{\hrule width=5.11108pt}\hbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}}\,,0,00
.
Assume that
(G,E)
satisfies
(Fin)
. Then

\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}=\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}=\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}.

These algebras are equipped with canonical
faithful contractive projections
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\twoheadrightarrow\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)
and

\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\twoheadrightarrow\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})
, and

(1)

\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
is maximal abelian if and only if
(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
hold;

(2)

\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{0}
is maximal abelian if and only if
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{00}
is maximal abelian if and only if

(Evr)
hold;

(3)

for any of the inclusions
C_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}^{P}(G,E,\sigma),\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)_{0},\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}^{P}(G,E,\sigma)_{00}
being maximal abelian is equivalent to
(Evr)
and
(Rec)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.13
the groupoids
\mathcal{G}(G,E)_{*}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)_{*}
are Hausdorff.
Hence, the first part follows. In particular, the algebras
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
and
\mathcal{T}^{P}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
are reduced Banach algebras of

\mathcal{G}(G,E)_{*}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)_{*}
, respectively, cf. Remark
2.14
.
Thus statements
(1)
–
(3)
follow from Theorem
6.24
and

[
BKM
]
*Proposition 5.11, cf. Theorem
2.23
(3)
.
∎

Corollary 9.34
.

Theorem
F
in the introduction holds.

Proof.

Composing the canonical generalised expectations with quotients by meager support functions we get contractive maps

\mathbb{E}:\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\to\mathcal{D}(\partial E)
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}\to\mathcal{D}(E^{\leq\infty})
,
which are pseudo-expectations in the sense of
[
KwM22
]
.
By
[
KwM22
]
*Theorem 3.6 and
[
KwM20
]
*Corollary 7.6
a Cartan
C^{*}
-inclusion has a unique pseudo-expectation and so it has to be the genuine faithful expectation onto
the masa subalgebra. Thus, if
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
or
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
is Cartan
then either
\mathbb{E}
takes values in
\textup{C}_{0}(\partial E)
or
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}
takes values in
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{\leq\infty})
.
The latter is equivalent to that either
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
or
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
is Hausdorff, but each of these alternatives is equivalent
to
(Fin)
by Proposition
6.13
. This shows necessity of
(Fin)
.
The remaining part of the assertion of Theorem
F
follows directly from Theorem
9.33
.
∎

Theorem 9.35
.

Let
(G,E,\sigma)
be a twisted self-similar groupoid action and let
\varnothing\neq P\subseteq[1,\infty]
.

(1)

If
(G,E)
is cofinal and satisfies
(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
, then
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
is simple.
If in addition it satisfies
(Con)
, then
\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
is purely infinite simple.

(2)

\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)
is simple if and only if
(G,E)
is cofinal, satisfies
(Evr)
,
(Cyc)
and the canonical map
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)\to\mathcal{O}^{P}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E)

is injective. If
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)
is simple and
(Con)
holds,
then
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)
is purely infinite.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.24
,

(Evr)
and
(Cyc)
is equivalent to topological freeness of
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
.
By Proposition
6.32
,
(G,E)
is cofinal if and only if
\mathcal{G}(G,E)

is minimal. Hence, the assertion follows from
Theorem
2.23
(4)
,
(5)
and
Proposition
5.34
.
∎

Corollary 9.36
.

Corollary
J
in the introduction holds.

Proof.

By
[
MiS
]
*Corollary 2.19 and Corollary
6.23

the groupoid
\mathcal{G}(G,E)
is amenable and finitely non-Hausdorff. Hence the equivalence
(o1)
\Leftrightarrow
(o2)

in Theorem
H

implies that simplicity of
\mathcal{O}^{P}(G,E)
is equivalent to the set of conditions
(Evr)
,
(Cyc)
,
(Min)
and
(Hum)
.
This set of conditions is equivalent to simplicity of
A_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{G}(G,E))
by
[
SS21
]
*Theorem A’ and

[
BGHL
]
*Theorem 4.2, cf. Table
1
and Theorem
A
.
∎

10.
The
C^{*}
-correspondence analysis

Throughout this section we fix a twist
\sigma=(\sigma_{G},\sigma_{\bowtie})
for a self-similar action
(G,E)
.
Here, we specialise our analysis to the case
P=\{2\}
and so the associated Banach algebras become
C^{*}
-algebras and representations
can be considered on Hilbert spaces or in
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebras. As it is customary, and as we did in the introduction, in this context we omit writing the subscript
\{2\}
.
Thus, we write
\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)\coloneqq\mathcal{T}^{2}(G,E,\sigma)
and
\mathcal{O}(G,E,\sigma)\coloneqq\mathcal{O}^{2}(G,E,\sigma)
for
the
Toeplitz
C^{*}
-algebra
and the
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebra
of
(G,E,\sigma)
, respectively,
and we adopt a similar convention for
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebras

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
,

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
,

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
covered by Definition
9.11
.
We will model these algebras as relative Cuntz–Pimsner algebras.

We recall that a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence
from a
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebra
A
to a
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebra
B
is a right Hilbert
B
-module
X
together with a left action of
A
implemented by
a
*
-homomorphism
\phi:A\to\mathcal{L}(X)
into the
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebra of adjointable operators on
X
, see
[
Lan95
,
BMR24
]
.
A
frame
for a right Hilbert
A
-module
X
is a family
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq X
such that for each
\xi\in X
, we have
\xi=\sum_{i}x_{i}\cdot\langle x_{i}\mid\xi\rangle_{A}
with convergence in norm.
When
A=B
we say
X
is a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence over
A
. We will unify the following two examples:

Example 10.1
.

The
graph correspondence

X(E)
of
E=(E^{0},E^{1},\textbf{{r}},\textbf{{s}})
is a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence over
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
. It is the completion of
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1})
in the norm induced by the
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
-valued inner product

\langle\xi\mid\eta\rangle(v)=\sum_{\textbf{{s}}(e)=v}\overline{\xi(e)}\eta(e),\quad\text{for }\xi,\,\eta\in C_{c}(E^{1})\text{ and }v\in E^{0},

with left and right actions of
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
determined by

a\cdot\xi\cdot b(e)=a(\textbf{{r}}(e))\xi(e)b(\textbf{{s}}(e)),\quad\text{for }\xi\in C_{c}(E^{1}),\,a,b\in\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0}),\text{ and }e\in E^{1}.

The point mass functions on edges
\{\mathbbm{1}_{e}\}_{e\in E^{1}}

form a frame for
X(E)
.

Example 10.2
.

Let
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
be a
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebra obtained as a Hausdorff completion of the
\sigma_{G}
-twisted convolution
*
-algebra
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})

in some
\textup{C}^{*}
-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
. We may treat
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
as a trivial
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence over itself, with the inner product
\langle a\mid b\rangle\coloneqq a^{*}b
,
a,b\in\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
Let
\{\mathbbm{1}_{g}\}_{g\in G}
be point mass functions on arrows of
G
. The image of
\{\mathbbm{1}_{x}\}_{x\in G^{0}}
in
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})

is a frame for
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

Remark 10.3
.

We may link the above examples using that
E^{0}=G^{0}
. Namely, the inclusion
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{0})\subseteq\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})

induces a
*
-homomorphism from
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
to a Hausdorff completion
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
of
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
Thus, we may view
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
as a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
to
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
, and we may form
the (internal) tensor product

X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G}).

This is naturally a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})
to
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
It follows from
[
BMR24
]
*Proposition 2.16 that the image of

\{\mathbbm{1}_{e}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(e)}\}_{e\in E^{1}}
in
X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
is a frame.
This frame is
orthogonal
in the sense that

\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(e)}\mid\mathbbm{1}_{f}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(f)}\rangle=[e=f]\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(e)},\qquad e,f\in E^{1}.

We may use it to define a left action of
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
on
X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
,
whenever the completion
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
is “self-similar” in the following sense.

Definition 10.4
.

An algebraic
correspondence over a pre-
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebra
A_{0}
, cf.
[
Nek04
]
*Definition 3.1, is an
A_{0}
-bimodule
X_{0}
together with a right
A_{0}
-valued inner product such that
\langle a\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle\xi,a^{*}\eta\rangle
, for
a\in A_{0}
,
\xi,\eta\in X_{0}
. For any
\textup{C}^{*}
-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
on
A_{0}
we may consider Hausdorff completions
A_{\lambda}
and
X_{\lambda}
of
A
and
X
in
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
and
\sqrt{\|\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\|_{\lambda}}
, respectively. This produces a right Hilbert
A_{\lambda}
-module
X_{\lambda}
, see
[
Lan95
]
*page 4, which may fail to be a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence over
A_{\lambda}
in the sense that the left action of
A_{0}
on
X_{0}
may not induce the left action of
A_{\lambda}
on
X_{\lambda}
. Extending
[
Nek04
]
*Definition 3.4, we say that the
\textup{C}^{*}
-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
is
self-similar
for
X_{0}
if
X_{\lambda}
is a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence over
A_{\lambda}
, that is if

\|\langle a\xi,a\xi\rangle\|_{\lambda}\leq\|a\|_{\lambda}^{2}\cdot\|\langle\xi,\xi\rangle\|_{\lambda}
for
a\in A_{0}
,
\xi\in X_{0}
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
X
be a
C^{*}
-correspondence over
A
, which is a completion of a correspondence
X_{0}
over
A_{0}
.
Let
A_{\lambda}
be a Hausdorff completion of
A_{0}
in a
\textup{C}^{*}
-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
not exceeding the one on
A
.
Then
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
is self-similar if and only if the kernel
I
of the canonical
*
-epimorphism

A\twoheadrightarrow A_{\lambda}
is positively
X
-invariant, i.e.
IX\subseteq XI
.

Proof.

Note that
A_{\lambda}\cong A/I
and recall that
X/XI
is naturally a right Hilbert
A/I
-module with the structure induced by
the quotient maps
q^{XI}:X\to X/XI
and
q^{I}:A\twoheadrightarrow A/I
, cf.
[
FMR03
]
*Lemma 2.1. In particular,
for
f\in X_{0}
we get
\|f\|_{\lambda}^{2}=\|\langle f,f\rangle\|_{\lambda}=\|q^{I}(\langle f,f\rangle)\|=\|q^{XI}(f)\|^{2}
. Thus,
X_{0}\ni f\mapsto q^{XI}(f)\in X/XI

induces an isometry
X_{\lambda}\to X/XI
, and it is easy to see that in fact it is an isomorphism of Hilbert modules
X_{\lambda}\cong X/XI
.
The left action of
A
on
X/XI
descends to a well-defined action of
A_{\lambda}\cong A/I
if and only if
I
is positively
X
-invariant, cf.
[
FMR03
]
*Lemma 2.3
∎

Below we use a convention that an expression
[sentence]
is zero if
the
sentence
is false and 1 otherwise. We denote by
\{\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\}_{(e,g)\in E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-6.77477pt{}\kern 7.05254pt{}}}_{{\kern-8.86238pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 7.05254pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-6.77477pt{}\kern 7.05254pt{}}}_{{\kern-8.86238pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 7.05254pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.08026pt{}\kern 4.35803pt{}}}_{{\kern-5.71428pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.35803pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-3.30247pt{}\kern 3.58025pt{}}}_{{\kern-4.9365pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 3.58025pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G}

the obvious linear basis for
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
.

Proposition 10.6
.

The space
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
is an algebraic correspondence over
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})

with operations given on basis elements by the formulas

(10.7)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{h}

\displaystyle=[\textbf{{r}}(h)=\textbf{{s}}(g)]\sigma_{G}(g,h)\mathbbm{1}_{e,gh},

(10.8)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\mid\mathbbm{1}_{f,h}\rangle

\displaystyle=[e=f]\overline{\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1}h)}\ \mathbbm{1}_{g^{-1}h},

(10.9)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{g}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{e,h}

\displaystyle=[\textbf{{s}}(g)=\textbf{{r}}(e)]\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\sigma_{G}(g|_{e},h)\mathbbm{1}_{ge,g|_{e}h}

for all
(e,g),(f,h)\in E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G
.
The maximal
\textup{C}^{*}
-norm on
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
is self-similar, that is
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
completes to a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence
X(G,E,\sigma)

over
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
. The map
\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{e}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{g}
extends to an isomorphism of right Hilbert
\textup{C}^{*}
-modules

(10.10)

X(G,E,\sigma)\cong X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G}).

Proof.

It is straightforward to see that the map
\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\mapsto\mathbbm{1}_{e}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{g}
extends to a linear isomorphism

\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}\operatorname{span}\{\mathbbm{1}_{e}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{g}\mid(e,g)\in E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G\}\subseteq X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
Thus, we use it to identify
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
with the dense subspace of
X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
Under this identification one readily sees that (
10.7
) holds.
For all
(e,g),(f,h)\in E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G
with
\textbf{{r}}(h)=\textbf{{r}}(g)
the
2
-cocycle conditions (
1.9
) for
\sigma_{G}
give

\sigma_{G}(g^{-1},g)=\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1})=\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1}h)\sigma_{G}(g^{-1},h)
.
Hence,

(10.11)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\mid\mathbbm{1}_{f,h}\rangle

\displaystyle=[e=f]\mathbbm{1}_{g}^{*}\mathbbm{1}_{h}=[e=f]\overline{\sigma_{G}(g^{-1},g)}\sigma_{G}(g^{-1},h)\mathbbm{1}_{g^{-1}h}

\displaystyle=[e=f]\overline{\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1}h)}\ \mathbbm{1}_{g^{-1}h}.

This shows that formulas (
10.7
) and (
10.8
) determine the structure
of pre-Hilbert
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
-module on
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
whose completion induced by the universal norm on
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})

is a Hilbert
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
-module
X(G,E,\sigma)
for which the isomorphism (
10.10
) holds.

Thus, we identify
X(G,E,\sigma)
with
X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
. We need to show that
(
10.9
) determines a homomorphism
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})\to\mathcal{L}(X(G,E,\sigma))
.
By universality of
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
, this boils down to showing that (
10.9
)
determines a
\sigma_{G}
-twisted unitary representation
g\mapsto U_{g}
of
G
in
\mathcal{L}(X(G,E,\sigma))
.
To this end, we use the orthogonal frame
\{\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\}_{e\in E^{1}}
for
X(G,E,\sigma)
, cf. Remark
10.3
.
For each
g\in G
, the multiplication by
\mathbbm{1}_{g}
, given by (
10.9
), defines a linear operator
U_{g}:\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)\to\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
which is a right
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
-module map. In fact, we have

(10.12)

U_{g}(\eta)=\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\mathbbm{1}_{g\cdot e,g|_{e}}\cdot\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\mid\eta\rangle,\qquad\eta\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G).

In particular,
U_{g}
is uniquely determined by its values on the frame
\{\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\}_{e\in E^{1}}
–
it is a unique linear right
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
-module map such that

U_{g}(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})=\mathbbm{1}_{g}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}=[\textbf{{s}}(g)=\textbf{{r}}(e)]\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\mathbbm{1}_{ge,g|_{e}}
.
For any
(g,h)\in G^{2}
and
e\in E^{1}
we have

\displaystyle U_{g}U_{h}\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar3}}}{{=}}U_{g}[\textbf{{s}}(h)=\textbf{{r}}(e)]\sigma_{\bowtie}(h,e)\mathbbm{1}_{he,h|_{e}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar3}}}{{=}}[\textbf{{s}}(h)=\textbf{{r}}(e)]\sigma_{\bowtie}(h,e)\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,he)\sigma_{G}(g|_{he},h|_{e})\mathbbm{1}_{ghe,g|_{he}h|_{e}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ssc_edges_G}}}{{=}}[\textbf{{s}}(gh)=\textbf{{r}}(e)]\sigma_{\bowtie}(gh,e)\sigma_{G}(g,h)\mathbbm{1}_{ghe,gh|_{e}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar3}}}{{=}}\sigma_{G}(g,h)U_{gh}\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}.

Hence,
U_{g}U_{h}=\sigma_{G}(g,h)U_{gh}
. Moreover, for any
e,f\in E^{1}

\displaystyle\langle U_{g}(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})\mid\mathbbm{1}_{f,\textbf{{s}}(f)}\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar3}}}{{=}}[\textbf{{s}}(g)=\textbf{{r}}(e)]\overline{\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{ge,g|_{e}}\mid\mathbbm{1}_{f,\textbf{{s}}(f)}\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar2}}}{{=}}[\textbf{{s}}(g)=\textbf{{r}}(e)][ge=f]\overline{\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)}\,\overline{\sigma_{G}(g|_{e},(g|_{e})^{-1})}\mathbbm{1}_{(g|_{e})^{-1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:inverse_vs_restriction}}}{{=}}[\textbf{{s}}(g)=\textbf{{r}}(e)][ge=f]\overline{\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)}\,\overline{\sigma_{G}(g|_{e},g^{-1}|_{ge})}\mathbbm{1}_{g^{-1}|_{ge}}.

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ssc_edges_G}}}{{=}}[\textbf{{s}}(g^{-1})=\textbf{{r}}(f)][e=g^{-1}f]\overline{\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1})}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g^{-1},f)\mathbbm{1}_{g^{-1}|_{f}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar2}}}{{=}}[\textbf{{s}}(g^{-1})=\textbf{{r}}(f)]\overline{\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1})}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g^{-1},f)\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\mid\mathbbm{1}_{g^{-1}f,g^{-1}|_{f}}\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar3}}}{{=}}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\mid\overline{\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1})}U_{g^{-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{f,\textbf{{s}}(f)}\rangle.

Using that
\{\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\}_{e\in E^{1}}
is a frame,
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
is a
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma)
-valued sesquilinear form, and
U_{g}
and
U_{g^{-1}}
are right
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma)
-module maps, one concludes that

\displaystyle\langle U_{g}(\zeta)\mid\eta\rangle=\langle\zeta\mid\overline{\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1})}U_{g^{-1}}\eta\rangle,\qquad\text{for any $\zeta,\eta\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)$}.

The relations we proved, show that for any
\eta\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
we have

\displaystyle\langle U_{g}(\eta)\mid U_{g}(\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle U_{g}(\eta)\mid\overline{\sigma_{G}(g,g^{-1})}U_{g^{-1}}U_{g}(\eta)\rangle=\langle\eta\mid U_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}(\eta)\rangle.

Since
U_{\textbf{{s}}(g)}
is an orthogonal projection onto the subspace
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
in
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
,
it extends to the contractive projection in
\mathcal{L}(X(G,E,\sigma))
. Thus, it follows that
U_{g}
extends to an adjointable partial isometry on
X(G,E,\sigma)

and the map
G\ni g\mapsto U_{g}\in\mathcal{L}(X(G,E,\sigma))
is a
\sigma_{G}
-twisted unitary representation of
G
.
∎

Remark 10.13
.

The correspondence structure on
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
in terms of functions
\xi,\eta\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
,
a\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
,
is given by
the formulas, for
(e,g)\in E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G
,

\displaystyle(\xi\cdot a)(e,g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in G\textbf{{s}}(g)}\sigma_{G}(gh^{-1},h)\xi(e,gh^{-1})a(h),

\displaystyle\langle\xi\mid\eta\rangle(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(e,h)\in E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-6.77477pt{}\kern 7.05254pt{}}}_{{\kern-8.86238pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 7.05254pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-6.77477pt{}\kern 7.05254pt{}}}_{{\kern-8.86238pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 7.05254pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.08026pt{}\kern 4.35803pt{}}}_{{\kern-5.71428pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.35803pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-3.30247pt{}\kern 3.58025pt{}}}_{{\kern-4.9365pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 3.58025pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G\textbf{{s}}(g)}\overline{\sigma_{G}(hg^{-1},g)}\,\overline{\xi(e,hg^{-1})}\eta(e,h),

\displaystyle(a\cdot\xi)(e,g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in\textbf{{r}}(g)G}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\sigma_{G}(h|_{h^{-1}e},(h|_{h^{-1}e})^{-1}g)a(h)\xi(h^{-1}e,(h|_{h^{-1}e})^{-1}g).

However, as we noticed in the proof above, it is determined by its values on the frame
\{\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\}_{e\in E^{1}}

and then even the formulas (
10.7
)-(
10.9
) simplify.

Corollary 10.14
.

Let
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
be a Hausdorff completion of
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G,\sigma_{G})
in a
\textup{C}^{*}
-seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
. Let
X_{\lambda}(G,E,\sigma)
be the corresponding Hausdorff completion of
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
,
and let
I_{\lambda}
be the kernel of the canonical
*
-epimorphism
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})\to\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
We have natural isomorphisms of right Hilbert
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
-modules

X_{\lambda}(G,E,\sigma)\cong X(G,E,\sigma)/X(G,E,\sigma)I_{\lambda}\cong X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})

and
X_{\lambda}(G,E,\sigma)
is naturally a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
to
\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
The seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
is self-similar if and only if the ideal
I_{\lambda}
is positively
X(G,E,\sigma)
-invariant.

Proof.

That the identity on
\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
induces an isomorphism
X_{\lambda}(G,E,\sigma)\cong X(E)\otimes_{\textup{C}_{0}(E^{0})}\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})
is
straightforward. In particular, for

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G)
where
a_{i}\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(E^{1})
and
b_{i}\in\textup{C}_{\textup{c}}(G)
we have

\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\|_{\lambda}^{2}=\|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\langle a_{i}\otimes b_{i},a_{j}\otimes b_{j}\rangle\|_{\lambda}=\|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}b_{i}^{*}\langle a_{i},a_{j}\rangle b_{j}\|_{\lambda}=\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\|_{X(E)\otimes\textup{C}^{*}_{\lambda}(G,\sigma_{G})}^{2}
. The remaining part follows from Proposition
10.6
and (the proof of) Lemma
10.5
.
∎

Let
X
be a
\textup{C}^{*}
-correspondence over a
\textup{C}^{*}
-algebra
A
.
Recall that a
representation of
X
in a
C^{*}
-algebra
B
is a pair
(\pi,\psi)
where
\pi:A\to B
is a representation and
\psi:X\to B
is a linear (necessarily contractive) map such that

\psi(a\cdot x)=\pi(a)\psi(x),\quad\psi(x\cdot b)=\psi(x)\pi(b),\quad\psi(x)^{*}\psi(y)=\pi(\langle x,y\rangle_{A}),

for all
a,b\in A,\,x\in X
.
When
B=\mathbb{B}(H)
for a Hilbert space
H
, we say that
(\pi,\psi)
is a representation on the Hilbert space
H
.
The
C^{*}
-algebra generated by
\pi(A)\cup\psi(X)
is denoted by
C^{*}(\pi,\psi)
.
The
Toeplitz algebra
of
X
is
\mathcal{T}(X)\coloneqq C^{*}(i_{A},i_{X})
where
(i_{A},i_{X})
is a universal representation of
X
in the sense that
for any other representation
(\pi,\psi)
the maps
i_{A}(a)\mapsto\pi(a)
and
i_{X}(x)\mapsto\psi(x)
determine a representation
\mathcal{T}(X)\to C^{*}(\pi,\psi)
.
Recall that (generalised) compact operators
\mathbb{K}(X)
is the closed linear span of rank one operators on
X
given by

\Theta_{x,y}(z)\coloneqq x\langle y,z\rangle_{A}
, for
x,y,z\in X
.
Any representation
(\pi,\psi)
of
X
in
B
induces a representation
\psi^{(1)}:\mathbb{K}(X)\to B
where
\psi^{(1)}(\Theta_{x,y})=\psi(x)\psi(y)^{*}
,
x,y\in X
.
For any ideal
J
in
J(X)\coloneqq\phi^{-1}(\mathbb{K}(X))
, we say that a representation
(\pi,\psi)
of
X
is

J
-
covariant
if
\pi(a)=\psi^{(1)}(\phi(a))
for every
a\in J
. The associated
relative Cuntz–Pimsner algebra
is

\mathcal{O}(J,X)\coloneqq C^{*}(j_{A},j_{X})

where
(j_{A},j_{X})
is a universal
J
-covariant representation of
X
.
It is equipped with the
gauge circle action

\gamma:\mathbb{T}\to\Aut(\mathcal{O}(J,X))

determined by
\gamma_{z}(j_{A}(a))=j_{A}(a)
and
\gamma_{z}(j_{X}(x))=zj_{X}(x)
, for
a\in A
,
x\in X
,
z\in\mathbb{T}
.
Note that
\mathcal{T}(X)=\mathcal{O}(\{0\},X)
.

Proposition 10.15
.

We have a bijective correspondence between representations
(\pi,\psi)
of the
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
-correspondence
X(G,E,\sigma)
on a Hilbert space
H

and
\sigma
-twisted representations
(W,T)
of
(G,E)
on
H
given by

\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})=W_{g}\quad\text{and}\quad\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})=T_{e}\quad\text{for all }g\in G\text{ and }e\in E^{1}.

Moreover,
(W,T)
is covariant if and only if
(\pi,\psi)
is
J_{\reg}
-covariant where

J_{\reg}\coloneqq\textup{C}^{*}(G_{\reg},\sigma_{G}|_{G_{\reg}})

sits naturally as an ideal in
C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

Proof.

Let
(W,T)
be representation of
(G,E,\sigma)
on
H
.
Since
\{W_{g}\mid g\in G\}
is a
\sigma_{G}
-twisted unitary representation of
G
, the universal property of
C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
induces a
*
-homomorphism
\pi\colon C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})\to\mathbb{B}(H)
such that
\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})=W_{g}
.
Recall that
\{\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\mid(e,g)\in E^{1}*G\}
densely spans
X(G,E,\sigma)
. Define
\psi
on this dense set by

\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{e,g})=T_{e}W_{g}.

If
(e,g),(f,h)\in E^{1}*G
, then

\displaystyle\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{g})^{*}\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{f,\textbf{{s}}(f)}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{h})

\displaystyle=W_{g}^{*}T_{e}^{*}T_{f}W_{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{\ref{defn:Cuntz--Krieger1}}}}{{=}}[e=f]W_{g}^{*}W_{\textbf{{s}}(e)}W_{h}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inverse_of_W_g}}}{{=}}[e=f]\overline{\sigma_{G}(g^{-1},g)}W_{g^{-1}}W_{h}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{\ref{enu:reps_of_twisted_self_similar1}}}}{{=}}[e=f]\overline{\sigma_{G}(g^{-1},g)}\sigma_{G}(g^{-1},h)W_{g^{-1}h}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inner_product_alternativ}}}{{=}}\pi(\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e,g}\mid\mathbbm{1}_{f,h}\rangle).

It follows that
\psi
extends to an isometric linear map
\psi\colon X(G,E,\sigma)\to\mathbb{B}(H)
satisfying
\psi(\xi)^{*}\psi(\eta)=\pi(\langle\xi\mid\eta\rangle)
for all
\xi,\eta\in X(G,E,\sigma)
.
The definition of
\psi
makes it clear that
\psi(\xi)\pi(a)=\psi(\xi\cdot a)
for all
\xi\in X(G,E,\sigma)
and
a\in C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
, cf. (
10.7
) and
(EP1)
,
(EP2)
.

For the left action, observe that for
(g,e)\in G*E^{1}
,

\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})=W_{g}T_{e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{\ref{enu:reps_of_twisted_self_similar3}}}}{{=}}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)T_{ge}W_{g|_{e}}=\psi(\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\mathbbm{1}_{g\cdot e,g|_{e}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar3}}}{{=}}\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{g}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}),

so
\pi(a)\psi(\xi)=\psi(a\cdot\xi)
for all
a\in C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
and
\xi\in X(G,E,\sigma)
.

Conversely, let
(\pi,\psi)
be any representation of
X(G,E,\sigma)
in
B(H)
and define
(W,T)
by the displayed formula in the assertion.
Then the map
g\mapsto W_{g}
is a
\sigma_{G}
-twisted unitary representation of
G
on
H
. In particular, the projections
W_{v}
,
v\in E^{0}
, are mutually orthogonal.
For
e,f\in E^{1}
we have

T_{e}^{*}T_{f}=\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})^{*}\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{f,\textbf{{s}}(f)})=\pi(\langle\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)},\mathbbm{1}_{f,\textbf{{s}}(f)}\rangle)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar2}}}{{=}}[e=f]\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{\textbf{{s}}(e)})=[e=f]W_{\textbf{{s}}(e)}.

Hence,
\{T_{e}\}_{e\in E^{1}}
are partial isometries with orthogonal range projections and
T_{e}^{*}T_{e}=W_{\textbf{{s}}(e)}
,
e\in E^{1}
.
Moreover, for each
(g,e)\in G*E^{1}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{g}T_{e}

\displaystyle=\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})=\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{g}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:universal_completion_is_self-similar3}}}{{=}}\psi(\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\mathbbm{1}_{g\cdot e,g|_{e}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{g\cdot e,\textbf{{s}}(g\cdot e)})\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{g|_{e}})=\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)T_{g|_{e}}W_{g\cdot e}.

It follows that
(W,T)
is a representation of
(G,E,\sigma)
. This proves the first part of the assertion.

Recall that
G=G_{\reg}\sqcup G_{\sing}
decomposes into a disjoint union of groupoids, see page
9.2
.
This implies that
\textup{C}^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})=\textup{C}^{*}(G_{\reg},\sigma_{G}|_{G_{\reg}})\oplus\textup{C}^{*}(G_{\sing},\sigma_{G}|_{G_{\sing}})

decomposes into a direct sum of
C^{*}
-algebras.
The expression (
10.12
) defining the representation
U
of
(G,\sigma_{G})
in
\mathcal{L}(X(G,E,\sigma))
, which is in fact the left action of the unitaries
\{\mathbbm{1}_{g}\}_{g\in G}
, implies that

(10.16)

\phi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})=\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\Theta_{\mathbbm{1}_{ge,g|_{e}},\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)}}

where the series is strongly convergent.
If
g\in G_{\reg}
, then the sum in (
10.16
) is finite and so
\phi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})\in\mathbb{K}(X(G,E,\sigma)
is compact.
Therefore,
J_{\reg}=\textup{C}^{*}(G_{\reg},\sigma_{G}|_{G_{\reg}})\subseteq\phi^{-1}(\mathbb{K}(X(G,E,\sigma))
.
Also for the corresponding representations
(W,T)
and
(\pi,\psi)
, for any
g\in G_{\reg}
we have

\displaystyle\psi^{(1)}(\phi(\mathbbm{1}_{g}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{ge,g|_{e}})\psi(\mathbbm{1}_{e,\textbf{{s}}(e)})^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}}\sigma_{\bowtie}(g,e)T_{ge}W_{g|_{e}}T_{e}^{*}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{enu:reps_of_twisted_self_similar3}}}{{=}}W_{g}\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}}T_{e}T_{e}^{*}=\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})\sum_{e\in\textbf{{s}}(g)E^{1}}T_{e}T_{e}^{*}.

Hence, we see that
(W,T)
is covariant if and only if
\psi^{(1)}(\phi(\mathbbm{1}_{g}))=\pi(\mathbbm{1}_{g})
for all
g\in G_{\reg}
if
and only if
(\pi,\psi)
is
J_{\reg}
-covariant.
∎

Corollary 10.17
.

For any twisted self-similar action
(G,E,\sigma)
we have natural gauge-invariant isomorphisms

\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)\cong\mathcal{T}(X(G,E,\sigma)),\qquad\mathcal{O}(G,E,\sigma)\cong\mathcal{O}(X(G,E,\sigma),J_{\reg}).

For each
*=\mathrm{red},\mathrm{ess}
, the above isomorphisms descend to isomorphisms
of
\mathcal{T}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)
and
\mathcal{O}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)
with some relative Cuntz–Pimsner algebras
associated to self-similar Hausdorff completions of
\textup{C}_{c}(G,\sigma_{G})
. In particular,

(1)

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
is a relative Cuntz–Pimsner algebra of a
C^{*}
-correspondence
X_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
associated to an exotic self-similar completion
C^{*}_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
of
\textup{C}_{c}(G,\sigma_{G})
and an ideal in the kernel of the canonical homomorphism
C^{*}_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})\twoheadrightarrow C^{*}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

(2)

If
E
is row-finite, then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
is a relative Cuntz–Pimsner algebra of a
C^{*}
-correspondence
X_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
over an exotic self-similar completion
C^{*}_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{ess}}(G,\sigma_{G})
of
\textup{C}_{c}(G,\sigma_{G})
and
an ideal in the kernel of
C^{*}_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{ess}}(G,\sigma_{G})\twoheadrightarrow C^{*}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
.

Proof.

The first isomorphisms follow from Proposition
10.15

and the universal description of the considered algebras.
By Remark
9.12
the algebras
\mathcal{T}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)
and
\mathcal{O}_{*}(G,E,\sigma)
, for
*=\mathrm{red},\mathrm{ess}
,
are equipped with canonical gauge-actions. Suppose that
(W,T)
is a representation of
(G,E,\sigma)
generating one of these algebras. Let

X_{W,T}\coloneqq\clsp\{T_{e}W_{g}:(e,g)\in E^{1}*G\},\qquad C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})\coloneqq\clsp\{W_{g}:g\in G\},

and

J_{W,T}\coloneqq C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})\cap\overline{X_{W,T}X_{W,T}^{*}}
where

\overline{X_{W,T}X_{W,T}^{*}}=\clsp\{T_{e}W_{g}T_{f}^{*}:(e,g,f)\in E^{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{r}}}}}}G\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-7.68211pt{}\kern 8.071pt{}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 8.071pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-5.24321pt{}\kern 5.6321pt{}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 5.6321pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}{\hphantom{{}_{{{\textbf{{s}}}}}}\times^{{\kern-4.15433pt{}\kern 4.54321pt{}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\textbf{{s}}}\kern 4.54321pt{\textbf{{s}}}}}}E^{1}\}.

Then
X_{W,T}
is a
C^{*}
-correspondence over
C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})
and
J_{W,T}
is an ideal in
C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})
that acts faithfully and by compacts on the left
of
X_{W,T}
. Hence, the relative Cuntz–Pimsner algebra
\mathcal{O}(J_{W,T},X_{W,T})
is isomorphic to the
given algebra by the gauge-invariance uniqueness theorem, see
[
Kak16
]
for instance.

(1)
. Assume that
(W,T)=(\widetilde{W}^{\mathrm{r},2},\widetilde{T}^{\mathrm{r},2})
is the representation on
\ell^{2}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E))
that generates
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
.
By Proposition
9.22
(1)
,
C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})
is
an exotic
C^{*}
-algebra of
\textup{C}_{c}(G,\sigma_{G})
. Note that
denoting by
P_{g}
the orthogonal projection onto the one dimensional space
\mathbb{C}\mathbbm{1}_{[\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g);\textbf{{s}}(g)]}
we get
that the formula

E(b)=\sum_{g\in G}P_{g}bP_{g}
,
b\in C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})
, defines a canonical conditional expectation
C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})\twoheadrightarrow c_{0}(G^{0})
.
Since every
T_{f}^{*}
, for
f\in E^{1}
kills every
\mathbbm{1}_{[\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g);\textbf{{s}}(g)]}
we see that

J_{W,T}\subseteq\ker E
. As the kernel of
C^{*}_{W,T}(G,\sigma_{G})\twoheadrightarrow C^{*}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})

is the largest ideal in
\ker E
the assertion follows.

(2)
. The same argument as in the proof
(1)
works,
as for row-finite graphs elements
[\textbf{{r}}(g),g,\textbf{{s}}(g);\textbf{{s}}(g)]
are in
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)_{\Hau}
, cf. the proof of Proposition
9.22
(1)
.
∎

Theorem 10.18
.

Let
(G,E,\sigma)
be a twisted self-similar groupoid action.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

G
is amenable;

(2)

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)
is amenable;

(3)

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E)_{0}
is amenable;

(4)

\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)
is nuclear;

(5)

\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)_{0}
is nuclear;

(6)

C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
is nuclear;

(7)

C^{*}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
is nuclear;

(8)

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)
is nuclear;

(9)

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)_{0}
is nuclear.

Assume these equivalent conditions hold. Then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}=\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
for
*=\textup{\mbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}\vbox{\hrule width=5.11108pt}\hbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}}\,,0
, and these algebras as well as
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)_{*}
,
for
*=\textup{\mbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}\vbox{\hrule width=5.11108pt}\hbox{\vrule height=1.72218pt}}\,,0
, are nuclear.
Moreover,
\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)
satisfies the UCT and in fact is
KK
-equivalent to
C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})
.
If, in addition, the graph
E
is row-finite, then

\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)=\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma).

Proof.

Assume
(1)
.
Then
C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})=C^{*}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
and they are nuclear by
[
BKMS
]
*Theorem 11.7
In particular, in the notation of Corollary
10.17
(1)

we have
C^{*}_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})=C^{*}(G,\sigma_{G})=C^{*}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,\sigma_{G})
and therefore
X_{\mathrm{t},\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)=X(G,E,\sigma)
,
cf. Corollary
10.14
. Thus,

\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)=\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
as they are both the Toeplitz algebra
\mathcal{T}(X(G,E,\sigma))
.
If
E
is row-finite then the same argument, and Corollary
10.17
(2)
,
gives also
\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)=\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ess}}(G,E,\sigma)
.
Applying
[
Kat04
]
*Theorem 7.2 to this Toeplitz algebra presentation we get
that
\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)=\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)
and
\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)_{0}=\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E,\sigma)_{0}
are nuclear.
Concluding
(1)
implies the conditions
(4)
–
(9)
.

By Corollary
10.17
,
\mathcal{T}(G,E,\sigma)\cong\mathcal{T}(X(G,E,\sigma))
. Hence,
(4)
–
(6)

are equivalent by
[
Kat04
]
*Theorem 7.2. They imply each of the conditions
(7)
–
(9)
, as nuclearity passes to quotients. By
[
Tak14
]
*Theorem 5.4,
(7)
and
(1)
are equivalent.
Hence,
(1)
and
(4)
–
(7)
are all equivalent.
Note that they are independent of twist since
(1)
is. Hence,
they are equivalent to nuclearity of
\mathcal{T}(G,E)
which is the groupoid
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(G,E))
by Theorem
9.7
. Therefore, these conditions are equivalent to
(1)
and to
(8)
by

[
BuM
]
*Theorem A or
[
BGHL
]
*Theorem F. Conditions
(8)
and
(9)

are equivalent because we have a conditional expectation
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{red}}(G,E)_{0}
, cf. Remark
9.12
.
Then
(9)
and
(3)
